| Blocked | The Web Design Museum's Flash puzzle-games page and the Medium copy of Kongregate's staff-picks post (both HTTP 403; the same post's second copy on kongregatedev.ghost.io did load, below); Wikipedia's Flash-games list, Flashpoint, Hapland, Tents and the NYT Connections article (404; the Sugar, Sugar page is the 1969 song, not the game, and the Mini Metro title is a disambiguation page) | Nothing; see the prompts below |
| Recalled, not opened | Queens' and Tango's rules, Tents, Sugar Sugar, Hundreds' and The Scale of the Universe's mechanics (Newgrounds' list names both, with years), Gorillas, cipher wheels | Marked **(recalled)** in the rows; recheck before a design depends on them |

**Searches for a wider reach** (these are the three prompts that produced the pasted lists in "Gemini's lists" below; more of the same would still help): (1) "Kongregate's, Armor Games' and Newgrounds' highest-rated puzzle, physics and casual (not action, not idle) games of 2007 to 2014, with a one-line mechanic each"; (2) "JayIsGames' best escape-the-room and tiny-world point-and-click games, and the classic Flash toys with no goal"; (3) "Coolmath Games' most-played logic and puzzle games, and the best single-player Nikoli-style puzzles on the web (rules, board sizes, whether solutions are unique)".

**What the sources show.**

- **Kongregate today is mostly idle games.** Its notable-games page lists ten titles with ratings only; the page gives no genres, but by my own knowledge (not stated there) Antimatter Dimensions and Idle Wizard are idle games, and the Wikipedia article's mobile list is idle-heavy. The incremental-game article calls the genre low-pressure with no fail state, and also describes it as "glorified spreadsheets" and notes that AdVenture Capitalist pioneered monetization and offline earnings.
- **The portal era is over, the games partly survive.** Miniclip closed its browser portal in July 2022 and kept two games; Coolmath moved its Flash games to the Ruffle emulator and states a policy of hosting only "non-violent" and "educational" games with no free chat, which is close to this project's own boundaries and shows an audience of children and young adults that wanted exactly that.
- **The classic hits are action.** Armor Games sponsored Kingdom Rush, Crush the Castle and GemCraft; Newgrounds' list of debuts includes many fighters, shooters and platformers (and some titles plainly out of bounds) beside a few quiet standouts (Hundreds, The Scale of the Universe, The Majesty of Colors); Fancy Pants is a momentum platformer with lives, enemies and bosses.
- **What fits is the quiet shelf:** *software toys* (Line Rider's creator calls it a toy: no goal or ending), element-combining puzzles (Doodle God), one-button calm games (Orisinal: small animals, ambient music, few with combat), two-player puzzles (Fireboy and Watergirl, aimed at ages 10 to 15), wordless tiny worlds (Samorost: no inventory, no dialogue) and no-goal builders (Townscaper: "more of a toy").
- **Cozy is a feeling, not a mechanic.** The Wikipedia article defines it as non-violence and relaxation with open-ended goals; its sources add "satisfying but not overly challenging" play. The project's rules already produce that, so the work is choosing mechanics, not adding a theme.
- **Parents already do a daily brain puzzle.** NYT Games had over 10 million daily players in 2024; Connections was played 3.3 billion times and Strands 1.3 billion times that year. LinkedIn's games share the pattern: daily refresh, completion time, streaks with freezes and twice-daily notifications. That appetite is the reason for the brain rows below; the daily/streak/notification half is exactly what the island refuses, and only the puzzle rules carry over.

**The house-rule screen.** Each genre the portals are known for, and what survives.

| Portal genre | Breaks | What survives |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Idle and incremental** (Kongregate's staple; Cookie Clicker, AdVenture Capitalist, Antimatter Dimensions) | Offline earnings make progress pile up while she is away ("nothing accrues, wears out or waits"); prestige resets and endless growth are an obligation loop | A **finite, in-session** shape with a real ending, as Candy Box! and Universal Paperclips have (the incremental article says both end), no offline progress, no resets, no purchases: see **Honey Hive** |
| **Tower defense and shooters** (Bloons, Kingdom Rush, Crush the Castle, Alien Hominid) | Combat, lives and pressure | Nothing new; Bubble Pop keeps the popping motion |
| **Daily puzzles** (NYT, LinkedIn) | Streaks, freezes, daily refresh, notifications, shared completion times | The puzzle rules only: Connections' four-mistake limit becomes unlimited tries; Queens and Tango as untimed boards (a time may be a second statistic, never the goal) |
| **Merge-by-physics** (Suika; its Chinese browser ancestor, Merge Big Watermelon, was January 2021) | A fruit over the top line ends the game | No end line: a full pond gently lifts; the island round ends on a goal ("make a swan") and "best size reached" is a grown-up personal best only: **Pond Merge** |
| **Network builders** (Mini Metro) | A station left overcrowded ends the game in Normal and Extreme | The Endless mode's rule, where stations never overcrowd, and the Creative sandbox: **Tram Lines** |
| **Aquarium sims** (Insaniquarium: fish die, aliens attack, shells are a currency, Time Trial) | Death, attack and a clock | Only the **Virtual Tank** (its personal aquarium): fish are never lost and nothing needs feeding |
| **Platformers** (Fancy Pants: extra life per 100 squiggles, bosses) | Lives and enemies | Already shelved as **Hop Course** (bumps count, no lives) |
| **Newgrounds' violent and dark titles** | Everything | Nothing |

**How these sources land on rows already in this notebook** (no second row for the same game):

| Source | Existing row | What it adds |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Fireboy and Watergirl | **Two Friends** (Flash and web) | A hazard that only one character can cross is the rule; on the island a wrong element bounces the friend back to the start of the room instead of a fatal touch. Hosted on Coolmath and sponsored by Armor Games, aimed at ages 10 to 15, which is the roadmap's proposed Maker Harbor (tween) age |
| Bookworm | **Letter Pond** (couch table) | Adjacent letters chained into words, with the cleared letters refilling. Its "books" (word categories that give bonus points once the first word is found) are a good untimed goal: find the garden words. Leave out the burning tiles that sink to the bottom |
| Spelling Bee, Letter Boxed, Word Whomp-style unscrambling | **Letter Pond** modes | Three more ways to use one reviewed word list: a honeycomb of seven letters with one required (rank bands, no streak), letters on the four sides of a square, and unscrambling one long word into all its small words. One list, three modes, one review |
| Townscaper | **Tile Village and Island Builder** (couch table) | The no-goal toy form: rule-driven blocks (a spire here, a balcony there) make arches and gardens she did not specify. The creator calls it a toy; an untimed, unscored mode of Tile Village |
| Unpacking, A Little to the Left | **Unpack the Room**, **Toy Drawer** | Already there; the cozy lists cite them over and over |
| Suika | **Number Merge**, **Snowball Merge** | A physics version of the same merge. `engine/ball.ts` has no ball-to-ball collisions (the Roll Table row records the same gap), so **Pond Merge** and Roll Table would share a new deterministic circle-pile solver. Cheap alternative: Number Merge on a grid |
| Orisinal, Samorost | **Gentle flash games** shelf, **Seed Order** | Four new rows below (Reflection Catch, Breezy Balloon, Dewdrop Worlds in the Flash and web table) |
| Line Rider | **Rainbow Road** (Flash and web) | Its maker's word, a *software toy*, is the right test for that row: no goal, and the physics has to be smooth enough to keep the rider on the sled |
| Insaniquarium's Virtual Tank | Treehouse | A new treehouse object, below |

#### New candidates for parents and older kids

Rows after the couch table's columns. **Home** says where it would live: *island* means the full island contract (a band, a how-to card, voice, one sticker); *grown-up* means a game for the `grownup` band; a grown-up game needs Lands stage B, `GameModule.inputs` and the touch-target decision before it can be on the map ([Touch, laptop and grandparents](#touch-laptop-and-grandparents-2026-10-09)), so until then its rules, generator and solver are the buildable part.

| Idea (after) | Play and skill | Smallest useful version, mistakes and rule checks | Effort · home |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Mix & Make** (Doodle God and Little Alchemy; opened: Doodle God starts with the four classical elements and 249 elements in 26 categories, with a hint every few minutes) | *Fun action:* drag two things together and see what they make; a seed and rain make a sprout, a sprout and sun make a flower, a flower and a bee make honey. *Skill:* cause and effect, science vocabulary, planning a chain of steps | A recipe graph of 40 to 60 code-drawn things (`props.ts` and `shapes.ts`). **Every pair does something**: a real result, or a friendly fizz and a funny "nothing yet" (no wrong answers, no red mark). Discoveries file themselves in the journal (`RoundResult.discoveries` already exists). A round is "find three new things" or "make a honey jar", A fizz is **not a miss**: free pairing is exploration. Only a named goal can miss (a goal pair never tried), and the glow after two fruitless tries on a goal is the usual hint and counts as one. Rule tests: every item is reachable from the starting four; each named goal has a shortest path (BFS), which is the grown-up **par** ("make honey in four mixes"); no recipe says something false (a science review of every pairing; Doodle God's figurative combos such as water plus fire making alcohol are not used). Young band: two ingredients, three results; school band: three-step chains and "how did you make it?"; grown-up window: complete the book, fewest mixes | M · island (pre-K to school) and a grown-up window |
| **Sort Four** (NYT Connections; the picture form continues Critter Sort → Critter Crossing → Rule Parade) | *Fun action:* sort sixteen cards into four groups of four that share a hidden reason; some cards seem to fit two groups until the rest settle. *Skill:* flexible categories, finding the shared attribute | Cards carry tags from the art that exists (things that fly, round things, things in a kitchen); a generated board is accepted only if a brute-force check finds exactly one way to make four valid groups, and each red herring fits a second group only until the others are placed. **Unlimited tries** (Connections allows four mistakes); a formed group speaks its reason; a hint lights two cards of one group. Picture cards for the island at 7 to 10; a word form for grown-ups needs the reviewed word list. No daily, no share grid | M · island school, then grown-up |
| **Islands and Sea** (Nikoli's Nurikabe; opened: each number is an island of that size, all the sea is one connected body, and no 2 by 2 pool) | *Fun action:* paint the sea around numbered islands. *Skill:* connectivity and deduction; the theme is an island, so the pet's own world | The article says a random grid is very unlikely to have one solution, so boards come from a **solver-checked generator or a frozen list**, as Garden Rows and Island Bridges do. Tap cycles blank, sea, dot. A broken rule shows as a gentle highlight (a puddle where a pool formed, a cut sea), never a loss; free undo; a hint fixes one cell by pure logic (a test: every board is solvable without guessing). 5 by 5 up to 10 by 10; past seven across needs the zoom decision | M · grown-up, with a school entry |
| **Garden Queens** (LinkedIn Queens, May 2024; **rules recalled**: one piece in each row, column and colored region, no two touching) | *Fun action:* place a bunny in every row, column and colored patch with no two touching. *Skill:* elimination | Region layouts from the seed, and a solver confirms a unique answer (a test). Tap once to mark "no", twice to place; free undo; 5 by 5 to 9 by 9. Completion time may be a second statistic, never the goal; no daily, streak, freeze or notification. Recheck the rule against the real puzzle before building | S–M · grown-up |
| **Two-flower beds** (Takuzu or Binairo; LinkedIn's Tango; opened: equal counts in each row and column, no more than two alike in a row, usually no identical rows) | *Fun action:* fill a bed with two kinds of flower. *Skill:* counting and forcing logic | **A mode of Garden Rows**, not a new game: same bed, same planting and removal. The rule is a pure function with a uniqueness test over generated boards. At 4 by 4 only equal counts and unique rows matter (three alike in a row cannot happen with two of each flower), so the no-three-in-a-row rule first bites at 6 by 6. 4 by 4 reaches pre-K to school; 6 by 6 and larger is the grown-up window | S–M · island and grown-up |
| **Tram Lines** (Mini Metro; opened: stations of different shapes, passengers want a matching shape, limited tunnels and bridges, Normal / Extreme / Endless / Creative modes) | *Fun action:* draw colored lines between stations so every shape can reach its match. *Skill:* networks, routes, transfers | **Start static:** a fixed map, pull a line out of a station, par = fewest lines or transfers found by search, as the couch courses do. Free play follows Endless (stations never overcrowd) and Creative (a sandbox). No clock and no overcrowding fail. Different decision from Island Bridges (links between islands) and Dot Link | M–L · island school and grown-up |
| **Petal Wheels** (Spirograph; opened: a small toothed wheel rolls inside a ring and a pen hole draws a hypotrochoid; Spirograph is Hasbro's trademark, so use a generic name) | *Fun action:* choose two gears and watch a flower draw itself. *Skill:* ratios, multiples and symmetry, as a drawing toy | An unscored **prediction first** ("how many petals?", as in Sink or Float), then the pen draws. The petal count is ring teeth over their greatest common factor with the wheel: a test checks that formula against a simulated trace for every gear pair. Pen offset moves the loops. A finished design can be kept in a picture slot (`creations.ts`); nothing is wrong, nothing is timed | S–M · island school (6 to 9) and grown-up cozy |
| **Decoder Ring** (cipher wheels and cryptograms; **recalled**) | *Fun action:* a friend's secret note arrives in odd letters and a two-ring wheel decodes it. *Skill:* alphabet order, patterns, deduction | A ladder: a symbol for each letter with a key shown, then "shift by one", "shift by three", an unknown shift found from a one-letter word or the first word, then a substitution with a few letters given. A test checks that every message decodes uniquely. The ring is a drag-to-rotate dial; a hint reveals a letter; a reviewed message bank and the same word list as Letter Pond; the pet reads the note once solved. Secret Code is the nearest game (colors, not letters) | M · island school (7 to 11) and grown-up |
| **Honey Hive** (the Kongregate idle shelf, made finite; Candy Box! and Universal Paperclips both end, per the incremental-game article) | *Fun action:* tap a flower, spend nectar on bees that gather by themselves, fill the hive. *Skill:* repeated addition and multiplication, rates, "which buy is better?" (cost per jar) | The island's answer to the genre, and the one that needs the developer's call (below). A round ends when the hive is full; **no offline progress, no resets, no real-money anything, no streaks, no waiting while away**; prices rise by a visible rule. Rule tests: a simulator proves every sensible buying order finishes the round within a stated number of taps, there is no dead end and no purchase that is strictly worse than another without being shown as such. Relates to Lemonade Stand's in-round purse and the proposed Harbor balance | M · island school (8 to 11) and grown-up |
| **Pond Merge** (Suika Game; opened: only the five smallest drop, matches merge into the next size, a fruit over the top line ends the game, no time limit; 11 million downloads by October 2024) | *Fun action:* drop ducklings and bubbles into a pond so equal ones merge into bigger ones. *Skill:* ordering by size, planning where things settle | **No end line.** The island round has a completion and one sticker, no score: "make a swan" or "fill the pond" ends it with a flourish, and a full pond gently lifts and lets the round go on. "Largest size reached" is a personal best in the grown-up window only. A free "take back the last drop". Needs a deterministic circle-pile solver (`ball.ts` collides circles with pegs and walls, not circles with circles), shared with Roll Table; a fixed-step replay so a bot and a course agree. Size chains are an ordering lesson (egg, chick, hen) | L · island pre-K to school and grown-up |
| **Virtual Tank** (Insaniquarium's personal aquarium, opened: it can double as a screensaver) | *Fun action:* put fish and shells into a window tank in the treehouse and watch them swim. *Skill:* none claimed; calm | A new treehouse object that shows stickers or kept creations as swimmers. Fish are never lost, nothing needs feeding, shells are not a currency. The frame only references a sticker, as the treehouse already does | S–M · island treehouse |
| **Zoom Walk** (The Scale of the Universe, Newgrounds 2010 and 2012; **mechanic recalled**) | *Fun action:* slide a bar from an ant to a whale to a mountain to the sun, tapping what you find. *Skill:* size, scale and powers of ten | A slider (not pinch, which small hands find hard) moving through code-drawn things at each scale; a name and a spoken size comparison on tap; an unscored "which is bigger?" prediction; journal entries. Every size fact is reviewed; real-world figures use rounded comparisons. [Space study](deep-dives/subjects/space.md): keep body-size and orbital-distance views distinct | S–M · island school (7 to 11) and grown-up |
| **Grow to a Hundred** (Newgrounds' *Hundreds*, 2010; **only the title and year opened, mechanic recalled**: numbered circles bounce and grow, and the numbers must reach 100) | *Fun action:* hold a bouncing circle to make it grow, leave it alone to let it bounce. *Skill:* part-whole to 100, estimation | A calm version: circles that touch simply stop growing and shrink back to their size, so nothing is lost. Fixed-step simulation, a target sum, and the grown-up par is the fewest holds. Needs a design pass against the real game before building | M · island school (6 to 8) |
| **Reflection Catch** (Orisinal's *Pocketful of Stars*; opened: a girl by a frozen pond whose reflection leaps to catch the reflected stars) | *Fun action:* stars fall only in the water; move the duck on the bank and its reflection catches them. *Skill:* mirror symmetry and the left-right-up-down of a reflection | One lake; a jump on the bank is a jump the other way in the water. A faint mirror line in level 1 and none later. Misses bounce; a star missed drifts back. Different from Mirror Brush (a painting) | S–M · island (5 to 8) |
| **Breezy Balloon** (Orisinal's *High Delivery*; opened: a small fan steers a vase-carrying balloon into falling roses) | *Fun action:* tap the fan to puff the balloon sideways and up. *Skill:* cause and effect of wind and force | A balloon that cannot pop (it bobs back); roses to gather; ties to the roadmap's wind weather | S · island (4 to 7) |
| **Dewdrop Worlds** (Samorost; opened: a gnome clicks things in a small world, no inventory and no dialogue, solving a screen moves him on) | *Fun action:* poke each part of a tiny world to find the order that sets things off. *Skill:* cause and effect, sequencing, without reading | A **one-world pilot** in the Windy Picnic pattern: three or four gadgets, a pet that walks on when it works, replayable, no wrong order (an unlucky order just starts again with a funny sound). Wordless, so the youngest readers play it. Art-heavy, so prove the loop with one world before a set | L · island (4 to 8) |

#### Smaller seeds from this pass

| Idea | First complete loop | Notes |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Toss Across** (QBasic's Gorillas; **recalled**) | Lob a snowball or a fruit over a hill to a hungry friend, change the angle and power, see the last arc left on screen | The nostalgic part is learning from the last shot, which is iterative estimation. Throwing *at* a gorilla is out; feeding a friend is in. Two players alternate on the couch. A Bouncy Launch or Peg Garden mode first |
| **Firefly Jar** | Fireflies drift toward a finger and scatter when she lifts her finger or taps the jar | A flocking toy for the lap band, with no goal: cause and effect only |


**Set aside from this pass, with the reason:** Cookie Clicker and other endless idlers (offline earnings and resets; see Honey Hive for the finite form), Bloons, Kingdom Rush, Crush the Castle and GemCraft (combat or pressure), Alien Hominid and the Madness series (violence), Fancy Pants (lives, enemies and bosses; Hop Course already holds the platformer), Insaniquarium's tank wars (death and attack), Papa's-style diner games (time pressure; Scoop Shop and Market Stall cover the orders), hangman (a hanged figure; a kind word-guessing form would be a Word Monsters mode), and every daily streak or leaderboard.

#### Gemini's lists (pasted by the developer, 2026-10-11)

The three lists the prompts above asked for came back as pasted text. **I opened none of the titles at a source**, so every mechanic below is Gemini's one-line summary, to recheck before a design depends on it. Things I would not rely on: Clicker Heroes is labeled a "non-idle active clicker", but the incremental-game article I opened names it an early pioneer of idle prestige mechanics, so it is an idle game; Cube Escape and the Rusty Lake series are not Flash-era (they are 2015 and later) and are horror, so they are out; "Doodragons / Draw-a-Thing" is not a title I can identify; and the "standard board sizes" (up to 20 by 20) are paper and desktop sizes, so on touch the target-size decision still applies and boards start at 5 to 8 across. The Nikoli claim that a properly made puzzle has exactly one solution reachable by logic matches the Nurikabe article I opened. The lists confirm the shelf found above (software toys, tiny worlds, spatial puzzles, logic grids) and add families the notebook did not have.

**Titles that land on rows already here:**

| Gemini title | Existing row | What it adds |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Sugar, Sugar | **Water Paths**, **Rainbow Road** | Drawn lines as fixed ramps, with color gates and cups that each want a count |
| Sushi Cat | **Peg Garden** | A pachinko drop with food on the way down |
| Magic Pen, Incredibots, Fantastic Contraption, World of Goo | **Wobble Works**, **Rainbow Road**, **Chain Reaction** | Already references |
| Super Stacker 2 | **Block Tower** | Already covered |
| Civiballs, Red Remover | **Snack Drop**, **Block Topple** | Red Remover's decision is *which block to remove first*. A kind form: remove props so the friends land in the pond and the others stay put. A seed, not a game |
| Electric Box | **Puddle Pipes**, **Light Lab** | Route a source to a target through placed parts |
| B-Cubed (step on every tile once) | [Mossy Path (built)](GAMES.md#mossy-path) | A second reference for the path-coverage game |
| 40xEscape | **Quick Tricks** | Forty one-screen logic tricks: the variety is the draw, and the "zero moon logic" bar Gemini credits to the good room-escape games is a design rule worth keeping |
| Submachine, Sphere, MOTAS, Vision, TRACE | **Escape Room** (Lantern Lake) | TRACE is on Coolmath, and its browser original was remastered for Steam in November 2025 (per the Coolmath article I opened) |
| The Grow series | **Seed Order** | Already there |
| 10 Gnomes (macro photographs) | **Hidden Pond** | Photographs cannot be imported; the code-drawn scene stands in |
| Tiny Bang Story, Chuchel, Samorost | **Dewdrop Worlds** | Already there |
| Silk | **Mirror Brush**, Song Maker | Symmetric glowing ribbons with soft chords: a Mirror Brush that plays |
| Shift | seed below | Pressing a button flips the world between two states |
| Suika at Coolmath | **Pond Merge** | Gemini says the merge game is among Coolmath's most played; not checked |
| Crush the Castle, Clicker Heroes | Set aside | Combat; idle |

**New from these lists:**

| Idea (after) | Play and skill | Smallest useful version, mistakes and rule checks | Effort · home |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Sand Table** ([dry-first research](deep-dives/subjects/earth-materials.md); dry rungs 1–3 [defined](definitions/sand-table.md) 2026-10-22, proposal, not built; broader seed remains later scope) (falling-sand toys: Powder Game, The Powder Toy; Gemini) | *Fun action:* pour sand, water and seeds on a canvas and watch them pile, flow and sprout. *Skill:* material properties and cause and effect; pure play for the youngest | Three materials and a sun: sand piles, water flows and soaks in, a seed with water sprouts. **Leave out fire, oil, gunpowder and lava**, the genre's destructive half. A grid of cells in fixed steps so it is deterministic; a test that grains are conserved and nothing moves forever; drawn through a render texture, so check the cost on an iPad. Lap: pour and watch. Preschool: fill the bucket, dam the stream. School: water a garden through a dam, with a journal entry per new reaction. Overlaps Fill and Dump (pouring), Splish Splash and Fossil Dig, none of which is a sandbox | M · island lap to school and a cozy grown-up window |
| **Streamsong** (Auditorium, Cipher Prime; Gemini) | *Fun action:* bend a stream of glowing particles with a few placed fields into cups; each full cup sings a note. *Skill:* cause and effect, planning a path; music as the reward | Particles from a source in fixed steps; one to three fields (tap to turn an arrow); a round completes when every cup is full, spilled particles recycle, nothing is lost. Notes from the pentatonic steps in `notes.ts`; a hint draws a ghost stream. Light Lab turns a beam with mirrors; this steers a flow with fields. Sound Garden is the music neighbor | M · island school and grown-up |
| **Pen Planner** (Shikaku; Gemini's rules: divide the grid into rectangles, each holding one number equal to its area) | *Fun action:* fence a field into pens. *Skill:* area, multiplication facts and arrays, which is a school standard | Boards made by tiling a grid and then numbering it, with a solver confirming one solution (a test). Drag a rectangle, undo; 5 by 5 to 8 by 8; pens for animals as the theme. Pairs with the round-three level "Make the pens fair", Bug Builder's area idea and the Rekenrek ideas. When defined it takes a name without "Meadow" or "Pen" (suggested Field Planner; [Meadow Pens definition](definitions/meadow-pens.md#identity-and-overlap)). Simon Tatham's open puzzle collection (named by Gemini; check its licence before borrowing anything) is a reference for generators | S–M · island school (7 to 10) and grown-up |
| **Lamp Lighter** (Akari or Light Up; Gemini's rules: light every white cell, no two lamps see each other, numbered walls count their neighboring lamps) | *Fun action:* place lamps and watch light spill along rows and columns. *Skill:* elimination | Solver-checked unique boards; a lamp's light is drawn as lines; a conflict dims both lamps and never stops the round; free undo; 5 by 5 to 8 by 8. A different decision from Light Lab (turning mirrors) and Lantern Lights (Lights Out) | S–M · island school and grown-up |
| **More grid types** (Slitherlink, Masyu, Fillomino; Gemini's rules) | Slitherlink: draw one closed loop where numbers count the edges used. Masyu: a loop through pearls with turning rules. Fillomino: split the grid into regions whose size equals their numbers | All three are unique-solution grid puzzles, each needing its own solver and generator; none needs new input beyond a tap or a drag. Slitherlink is the friendliest and sits near Dot Link; Masyu's and Fillomino's rules are unchecked. After Islands and Sea, Pen Planner and Lamp Lighter prove the solver pattern | M each · grown-up |
| **Log Roll extensions** (three grown-up levels built in Tinker Lab) | *Fun action:* tip a log end over end across stepping stones. *Skill:* spatial orientation and planning | First slice is built; switches, fragile tiles, school entry and controller play remain proposals. A breadth-first search gives the fewest rolls (a par and a hint) and a backward generator builds levels. Four arrows or swipes on touch; a d-pad fits the couch. The pet pushes a barrel, not a block | M · island school (6 to 10) and grown-up courses |
| **Caterpillar Climb** (Apple Worm; Gemini: a segmented worm eats apples to grow, then bridges gaps and climbs under gravity) | *Fun action:* nibble apples so the caterpillar stretches over a gap. *Skill:* planning with length and gravity | Grid puzzles solved by search; a fall settles softly on the pond at the last stand instead of ending anything. Neighbors: Inchworm Measure (length) and Penguin Slide (a solved grid) | M · island school and grown-up |
| **Echo Friends** (The Company of Myself and Chronotron; Gemini: record a run and your shadow repeats it) | *Fun action:* walk one friend through a room, then play the other while the first repeats its walk. *Skill:* sequencing and timing, two actors from one player | **A mode of Two Friends**, and the answer to that row's requirement of a one-player switch: the past self is the second friend. A fixed-step recording replayed exactly (a test that the replay matches) and a small set of two-door rooms | M · island school and grown-up |

*Seed: **Day and Night rooms** (Shift).* A button flips a room between two states and some platforms exist in only one, with a bump that sends the pet back to the start of the room, never a loss. A mode of Echo Friends' rooms or Light Lab, not a game.

**Suggested order (non-binding).** Chosen for how much already exists to build on and how well a test can assert the rules:

1. **Mix & Make**: the journal, `props.ts` and `RoundResult.discoveries` exist; the rules are a graph a test can walk; it spans pre-K to grown-up.
2. **Sort Four, picture form**: a data-driven continuation of the Critter Sort ladder, with a uniqueness test that finds bad boards.
3. **Petal Wheels**: small, creative, one formula to test, and a keepable picture.
4. **Islands and Sea**: a solver-checked generator on a themed grid, reusing Island Bridges' grid and touch work; a grown-up logic game that also has an island entry for older children.
4b. **Pen Planner**: the same solver pattern, and its rectangle areas are a school multiplication standard, so it has the clearest learning purpose of the grid puzzles. **Sand Table** is the strongest new cozy toy (it starts at lap) and its reaction rules are a table a test can read.
5. **Garden Queens** and **Two-flower beds**: small brain boards on the same solver pattern, once a uniqueness solver exists for Islands and Sea.
6. **Honey Hive**: the answer to "what about Kongregate's idle games", worth building only if the developer decides a finite idler belongs (below).

**Decisions this needs from the developer.**

1. **Is a finite, in-session idle-shaped game acceptable at all?** The genre's pull is the compulsion loop the rules refuse. The proposal removes offline gains, resets, purchases and streaks and ends the round, but if even that feels wrong, drop Honey Hive and teach the same rates through Lemonade Stand and Market Stall.
2. **Where do the brain boards live before Lands stage B?** Either as couch games on the existing route (a frozen board list and a `couchgames` bot) or wait for the map. The lean: build the rules, generator and solver now, because they are the same either way.
3. **One reviewed word list** would serve Letter Pond's modes, Sort Four's word form and Decoder Ring's messages; it is a content review the developer or a reviewer has to own.
4. **Science and scale facts** (Mix & Make's recipes, Zoom Walk's sizes) need a reviewer before they are spoken to a child.

### Arcade classics and the Animal Crossing NES shelf (2026-10-11)

The request: another deep dive for game ideas, aimed mainly at parents and older children with brain-game, cozy or nostalgic qualities, starting from the arcade classics (Snake, Minesweeper, Pac-Man, Kaboom!) and the games you could play inside the GameCube *Animal Crossing*. Everything here is a **proposal**. The new-candidate rows follow the [Flash pass's](#new-candidates-for-parents-and-older-kids) columns (**home** says whether the island contract or the grown-up route applies).

**The first finding is overlap.** Four of the named classics already have a built game or a row, so the useful work is what the sources add to those rows and which few ideas are really new. The table below goes first for that reason.

**Access log: what I could and could not read.**

| Source | Result | Used for |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Opened and read (Wikipedia) | [Kaboom!](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaboom!_(video_game)), [Minesweeper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minesweeper_(video_game)), [Snake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Snake_(video_game_genre)), [Breakout](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breakout_(video_game)), [Qix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qix), [Lode Runner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lode_Runner), [Boulder Dash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boulder_Dash_(video_game)), [Balloon Fight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balloon_Fight), [Clu Clu Land](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clu_Clu_Land), [Donkey Kong Jr. Math](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donkey_Kong_Jr._Math), [Excitebike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Excitebike), [Ice Climber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ice_Climber) and [Animal Crossing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Animal_Crossing_(video_game)) | Facts stated as facts below |
| Opened, **first 100,000 characters only** | [Pac-Man](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pac-Man) and [Q\*bert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Q*bert) (each page is about 130,000 to 208,000 characters; ghost scatter and chase timing in Pac-Man was not reached) | Mechanics stated as facts, nothing about the unread remainder |

Slider's Saturday concerts, golden tools for a complete collection | Background, labeled where used |

**Titles that land on rows already here.**

| Classic | Existing row or game | What the research adds |

| Kaboom! | **Egg Catch** (built; [GAMES.md](GAMES.md#egg-catch) credits Kaboom!) | The Mad Bomber's face changes with how the player is doing, and critics singled it out for praise: a hen whose face reacts is the right kind of feedback. The ports added a **Pitch and Catch** mode where one player drops and the other catches: a two-player Egg Catch where the grown-up chooses where the eggs fall. The difficulty switches could halve the bucket's width: a visible basket-width setting is a clean handicap. A bucket lost per miss is the part already softened to hay |
| Snake | **Pond Conga** (built, couch) | The genre began as *Blockade* (1976), a two-player game; *Nibbler* (1982) put the snake in a maze; *Snake Byte* (1982) ends a level when the apples are eaten. A two-line Blockade-style duet on one pond, where a bump is a bonk, is the only unbuilt shape; check the couch face-off code first |
| Pac-Man | **Munch Maze** (90s edutainment table) | Sourced ghost rules and the design intent (Iwatani wanted a nonviolent game about eating; ghost personalities were added so it was neither boring nor impossible; intermissions were added to ease the tension). New: **Wisp Maze**, below |
| Minesweeper | **Set aside** at the end of the couch table | The set-aside note is now reopened (see **Garden Sweep**, below) |
| Breakout | **Brick Garden** (Atari table); **Pond Pinball and Brick Breaker** | The sourced setup is eight rows with bricks worth 1, 3, 5 and 7 by color, a ball that speeds up after four and twelve hits, a paddle that halves, and two screens before the game stops. The speed-up and the shrinking paddle are the pressure to leave out; a brick row with its own number to add is worth keeping |
| Q\*bert | **Color Hop** (Arcade table) | A ready-made ladder, sourced: one hop turns a cube; then **two hops** per cube; then cubes that **toggle** on every landing (so a second landing undoes the first); then both. Slick and Sam, who revert changed cubes, are harmless tidiers and fit a friendly helper; the spinning discs that carry the hero back to the top are the soft landing for the edge, which is fatal in the original |
| Dig Dug, Mr. Do! | **Garden Dig** (low priority) | Dig Dug pumps enemies until they burst or drops rocks on them: out. Mr. Do! is described as more about collecting than defeating, so the digging verb is safe and the enemies are not |
| Pengo | **Ice Push**; Penguin Slide level 6 (built) | Nothing new found (recalled only) |
| Lode Runner | None | Digging a hole that refills and kills a guard is out. What survives is the **level editor**, one of the earliest and praised for its ease, which is the same lesson as Excitebike's below |
| Balloon Fight | **Tap to Flap**, **Puff Float**, **Breezy Balloon** | Seed below: balloons as lift |
| Clu Clu Land | **Lantern Lights**, **Color Hop** | Seed below: odd passes |
| Donkey Kong Jr. Math | **Number Merge**, **Frog Hop** | **Make Twelve** (built 2026-10-13 as **Fruit Sums**; [GAMES](GAMES.md#fruit-sums)), below |
| Excitebike | **Ramp Race** | **Track Maker**, below |
| Ice Climber | **Cloud Hop**, **Ice Push** | Seed below: a cooperative climb |
| Golf, Pinball, Tennis, Baseball, Soccer | **Mini Putt**, **Pond Pinball**, **Bounce Back**, **Roll Table** | No new idea. Punch-Out!! is boxing: out |
| Animal Crossing's fish, bugs and fossils | **Fishing Pond**, **Bug Net**, **Fossil Dig**, **Magnet Fishing** | The structure is what is new: a museum page, a golden tool for finishing a collection, and Gyroids; see "What the Animal Crossing shelf teaches", below |
| Animal Crossing's house and garden | **Cottage Garden**, **Unpack the Room** | Nothing new |

**What survives the house screen.** Every classic named has a fail or a chase at its core, so the question each time is which part is the fun.

| Classic | The pressure at its core | What stays |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Kaboom! | Every miss costs a bucket and all the bombs explode | Moving under falling things; a reacting face |
| Snake | The body is the wall, and a collision ends the run | Steering with a growing tail and planning a path (Pond Conga already does it) |
| Pac-Man | A pursuit; this is the notebook's "countdowns and chasers" case | Distinct rules for each follower, and the maze (the hunt itself, only if turn-based; **decision 2**) |
| Minesweeper | One wrong guess ends it, and some boards force a guess | Counting neighbors and deduction (only on boards that never force a guess) |
| Boulder Dash | A timer, and falling rocks crush the digger | Gravity as the puzzle |
| Qix | Lives, Sparx on the edges and a fuse that burns back | Claiming a share of a field and judging how much |
| Q\*bert | Fatal enemies and a fatal edge | The hop-and-recolor rule ladder |

**New candidates for parents and older kids.**

| Idea (after) | Play and skill | Smallest useful version, mistakes and rule checks | Effort · home |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Make Twelve** (**first slice built 2026-10-13 as Fruit Sums**, school levels 1–3: add to 10, add to 20, then plus or take away; see [GAMES](GAMES.md#fruit-sums). Still proposals here: three-fruit sums, times, the grown-up "make 24" and two players sharing a tray) (Donkey Kong Jr. Math's *Calculate* mode; opened: Donkey Kong holds up a target number, and the players climb vines to collect digits and signs and combine them to reach it; a second mode gives fixed sums; two players build toward the same number) | *Fun action:* climb a tree for number fruit and sign leaves, and build any sum that equals the number on the sign. *Skill:* number sense and choosing an operation, and seeing that many routes reach one answer | A tray of four to six number tiles and two or three signs. **Every equation that reaches the target is accepted**; a test checks each generated target has at least two different solutions from its tray and that no tray is unsolvable. A wrong total says what it made ("that makes 8") and lets her swap one tile; the hint lights one useful tile. Ladder: sums to 10, then 20, then subtraction, then three tiles, then times. Two players build toward the same target and share the tray, which is co-play with no turn to wait for. Numerals and signs are spoken, so no reading is needed. The four-number "make 24" form (recalled) is the grown-up window. Different decision from Monster Munch and Duck Pond (one operation each) and Frog Hop (a number line): here the choice of operation is the point | S–M · island school (6 to 9) |
| **Garden Sweep** (Minesweeper and tentaizu; opened: a number shows the mines in the eight neighbors, a zero opens its neighbors, the first click can be made safe (Vista's guaranteed opening), and some boards can only be finished by guessing; at about 25% mines the article describes a phase change after which boards cannot be solved without guessing; tentaizu starts with some cells shown, marks mines instead of opening cells, and usually has one solution) | *Fun action:* tap the soil to see how many sleeping moles are touching a square, and mark where they are. *Skill:* counting the neighbors and deduction | The theme replaces the mines with moles, and **a wrong tap pops a laughing mole up and carries on** (a miss, never a loss). The first tap and its neighbors are safe. Boards come from a generator accepted only when a solver finds every cell by logic with no guess (a test; density stays at about 12 to 20%, the range of the standard 9 by 9 and 16 by 16 boards, well under that phase change). Tapping a satisfied number opens the rest of its neighbors (chording) so one finger plays. Free undo; no timer, because the genre's record metric is a time and hints used is ours. Sizes: 5 by 5 with three moles up to 7 by 7 under the 100-unit rule; the standard 9 by 9 with ten and the 16 by 16 with forty (opened) need the zoom the [touch table](#touch-laptop-and-grandparents-2026-10-09) already discusses. The tentaizu form (reveal a few cells and mark every mole, one solution) is the quiet puzzle version. The notebook's old reason for setting Minesweeper aside was the wrong-guess loss and the forced guess, and this removes both | M · island school (6 to 8) and grown-up |
| **Mole Mine** (Boulder Dash; opened: dig dirt to collect diamonds, objects stay put until the dirt under them is gone and then fall, a quota opens the exit, each cave has a time limit, and solutions depend on timing and object interactions) | *Fun action:* tunnel through soil for acorns and shake rocks loose; open the door. *Skill:* gravity, cause and effect, planning a dig order | **Turn-based**, one step per tap, and rocks fall only by rule after each step. A rock that lands on the digger is a bump that takes back that one move, and the cave has no timer. A breadth-first solver proves every cave solvable and gives the fewest moves (the par and the hint). Caves from a frozen list or a backward generator. First caves have rocks only; the original's amoebas and butterflies stay out. Shares a verb with Garden Dig but not its learning, so keep it a separate, later row | M · island school (7 to 10) and grown-up courses |
| **Wisp Maze** (Pac-Man's ghosts; opened: Blinky chases directly, Pinky tries to get ahead of Pac-Man, Inky uses a more complicated plan, Clyde alternates between chasing and fleeing by distance; warp tunnels cross the edge) | *Fun action:* a maze where each tap moves the pet one step and then every lantern wisp takes a step by its own rule, shown on its lantern; gather the cookies and reach the door. *Skill:* following a stated rule, predicting a move ahead, planning | **Turn-based, so there is no clock and nothing to outrun.** A wisp that reaches the pet gives a hug that sends it back to its last cookie spot (never a loss), and undo is free. A rule is a pure function of positions (a test that the same board plays out the same way), and a solver over (pet, wisps, cookies) gives each level's fewest steps and a hint that shows the next safe step. Ladder: a wisp that stays put, one that follows, one that goes ahead of the pet, then two wisps with different rules. Warp tunnels become a trick the pet can use. This is the one idea here that touches "countdowns and chasers" in the notebook's what-not-to-borrow list, so **decision 2** asks | M · island school (7 to 10) and grown-up |
| **Meadow Fence** (Qix; opened: the player draws lines from the border to close off territory, the level ends at a set percentage (75% by default, adjustable from 50 to 90%), and the closed area fills with color; JezzBall, which splits a field with straight lines while balls bounce, is a **recalled** cousin) | *Fun action:* drag from the edge across a meadow to fence off a part, and it blooms. *Skill:* area, fractions and percent, estimating a share | One slow butterfly roams the meadow. When a fence closes, the piece **without** the butterfly is the one claimed, so it is never wrong; a fence the butterfly crosses while she draws springs back, with no fuse, no edge-walkers and no cost. An unscored prediction before each close ("more than half?"), then the share is shown as a fraction. Ladder: claim half, a quarter, three quarters, then tenths and percent. The meadow is a coarse grid so area is exact and a test checks the claimed shares sum to the whole and the butterfly is never inside a claimed piece. When defined it takes a name without "Meadow" (suggested Butterfly Field; [Meadow Pens definition](definitions/meadow-pens.md#identity-and-overlap)). Neighbors: Lasso Loops (drawing loops to count) and Pen Planner (areas of rectangles). Different decision: area claimed against a mover | M · island school (7 to 10) |
| **Track Maker** (Excitebike's Design Mode; opened: a track editor with 19 types of hurdles, saved tracks (32 on the 3DS), and called "the first of its kind in a console game", credited with greatly extending the game's life; Lode Runner's level editor is the same lesson) | *Fun action:* lay ramps, bumps and puddles along a track, then watch the pet's bike ride what she made. *Skill:* design, test and change one thing | A **mode of Ramp Race**, using its stated simple model so a track's result is predictable. Six slots and four pieces; every set of pieces finishes (nothing traps), a test. An unscored "will it make the jump?" before each ride. A kept track uses a bounded creation slot ([`creations.ts`](../src/content/creations.ts)) and can hang in the treehouse. A grown-up can design a track for her and she one for the grown-up (co-play, and the closest thing here to a Pitch and Catch). The same editor idea later suits Mole Mine: a cave the solver says is solvable in N moves | M · island school and Maker Harbor |
| **Doorbell Tune** (Animal Crossing's town tune; **unclear**: the Wikipedia page says a town tune comes from e-Reader cards at the Post Office, while I recall the player composing one in the game) | *Fun action:* make an eight-note jingle on a tiny staff and hear it as the treehouse's doorbell and when the pet arrives. *Skill:* melody, up and down, a first composition | Song Maker's pentatonic steps; eight slots kept in a tune slot (`creations.ts`), replaceable at any time. Nothing is lost if it is left alone and nothing is owed. Typed story results ([DESIGN.md](DESIGN.md#proposed-story-results)) would later carry it into a story | S · island treehouse |

**Smaller seeds from this pass.**

| Idea | First complete loop | Notes |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Rally** (NES Tennis, Pong; Kaboom!'s Pitch and Catch) | The pet and the child keep one ball going, and the count is theirs together | A cooperative rally count as a personal best, with no opponent. A mode of Bounce Back. A basket-width setting is the handicap, as in Kaboom! |
| **Cloud Climb** (Ice Climber; opened: eight layers of ice, a mallet to break the blocks overhead, indestructible blocks that force a detour, a bonus stage for vegetables, and cooperative play to climb) | Two climbers, one of them the grown-up, each break a block overhead to open the way up the mountain | The Topis that refill holes and the White Bear that arrives if you are slow are out, as is the 40-second bonus clock. Detours round the blocks that will not break make it a route puzzle. Needs a cooperative single-device input check before a design |
| **Odd passes** (Clu Clu Land; opened: she can only turn at posts, and in later stages an ingot flips each time she passes over it, so she must pass an odd number of times; Q\*bert's toggling cubes are the same rule) | A grid where each square flips each visit, and the goal is every square flipped once | Parity as the thing learned. A **mode** of Color Hop or Lantern Lights, not its own game. A solver confirms each board |
| **Balloon lift** (Balloon Fight's Balloon Trip; opened: two balloons give flotation, popping one makes it harder to rise, and a single-player mode collects balloons while dodging sparks) | The more balloons she holds, the higher she floats; collect balloons to reach a high cloud | Quantity as lift ("two balloons, plus three more") with no sparks or popping, as a mode of Tap to Flap. The sea that costs a life is a soft cloud |
| **Shadow Fish** (Animal Crossing's fishing; **recalled**: a fish's shadow in the water hints at its size before it bites) | A shadow swims by, and she guesses which fish casts it before it bites | A mode of the proposed Fishing Pond, an unscored prediction as in Sink or Float. Size comparison |
| **Treehouse Chorus** (Gyroids; opened: creatures by the player's house, and talking to one saves; **recalled**: each hums its own sound) | Small creatures, each with a pentatonic voice, placed around the treehouse make a chorus | A treehouse object, never a currency or a chore. Neighbor: Virtual Tank (the same "things she earned live in the room" idea) and Sound Garden |
| **Golden net** (Animal Crossing; excerpt: a golden version of the tool after catching every fish or insect species once) | Completing a journal page of Bug Net or Fishing Pond gives a gold-colored net in the treehouse | The one structural idea worth taking as it is: predictable (complete the page and you get it), cosmetic, nothing missed. Only after those games exist |

**What the Animal Crossing shelf teaches about structure.** The Nookipedia list shows about nineteen NES games, none of them sold as a game: they are found as furniture. Each is obtained by a different route: a raffle, a villager's birthday gift, Crazy Redd's stall, a letter from Nintendo, an e-Reader card, a secret code from a website, or from the linked island. Two (Super Mario Bros. and Zelda) cannot be obtained at all, one code in Japan was limited-time, and the list differs by region (the European game lost its e-Reader support, so Ice Climber cannot be had there). That is exactly the "opaque random rewards" and gating the project rules out, so none of it is copied. What is worth keeping is the **feeling**: a shelf of tiny classic games you stumble on inside a cozy home. **Decision 1** asks whether the older and grown-up games should look like that. Two other Animal Crossing structures stay out: the real-time events (a July fishing tournament, K.K. Slider on Saturdays) because nothing here expires or rotates (a "pond party" that is always open is the equivalent), and Tom Nook's mortgage, because a debt is an obligation. Gyroids, the museum and the golden tool are fine, because they are things you find or complete and nothing waits.

**Set aside from this pass, with the reason:** Kaboom!'s bucket loss and exploding screen, Snake's death on its own body, Pac-Man's real-time chase and power-pellet hunt (a ghost eaten is combat), Qix's lives and edge-walking Sparx, Dig Dug's pump and rocks, Lode Runner's guard-killing holes, Boulder Dash's timer, Balloon Fight's pop-and-drown fight, Punch-Out!!, and every high-score-and-extra-life loop. Considered and left to existing rows: Breakout, Q\*bert, Pengo, the sports games.

**Suggested order (non-binding).**

1. **Make Twelve** (first slice built 2026-10-13 as **Fruit Sums**, school levels 1–3; [GAMES](GAMES.md#fruit-sums)): the clearest learning purpose, a small rules module a test can walk, and it needs no new input.
2. **Garden Sweep**: the strongest new brain-and-cozy board; the generator and solver are the work, and they are the same pattern as Islands and Sea and Pen Planner.
3. **Color Hop** with the sourced ladder (one hop, two hops, toggling, both): a cheap, data-driven row once built.
4. **Meadow Fence**, then **Wisp Maze** and **Mole Mine** (each is solver-checked and turn-based).
5. **Track Maker** after Ramp Race's level ladder is settled; **Doorbell Tune** whenever a small treehouse change fits.

**Decisions this needs from the developer.** (Also collected under [Waiting on the developer](WAITING-ON-DEVELOPER.md#waiting-on-the-developer).)

1. **Should older and grown-up games appear as a "retro shelf" in the treehouse or home spot,** shaped like Animal Crossing's NES games (little cabinets or handhelds)? As presentation only, with every game open and found in a fixed place, never by raffle or gift. If not, nothing here depends on it.
2. **Is a turn-based follower puzzle acceptable** (Wisp Maze), given the rule against chasers? It has no clock and no loss, but the shape is a pursuit. If not, drop it and keep the odd-passes seed for route puzzles.
3. **Reopen Minesweeper as Garden Sweep?** The old note set it aside for its wrong-guess ending; the proposal removes that and the forced guess.

<a id="for-gemini-arcade-and-animal-crossing-pass"></a>**For Gemini (arcade and Animal Crossing pass).** Three prompts for what I could not read. As before, paste back what it finds and I will label it as unopened.

1. "For these arcade games: Pengo, Tapper, Joust, Mr. Do!, Dig Dug, Boulder Dash, Frogger, Centipede, Bomberman, Dr. Mario, Lode Runner: give each one's core rules in three lines, say whether the core verb is violent, and say what a calm, turn-based version of it could keep. Add the best cozy or brain-game cousins of each."
2. "For the 2002 GameCube *Animal Crossing*: how exactly do fishing (the shadow size, the bite and the tug), bug catching (the approach and the net), fossil digging and the museum, Gyroids, and composing the town tune work? Which NES games did it hold, and what is each one's core loop? Which activities make a good cozy loop for a four-to-ten-year-old, and which are about debt, schedules or scarcity?"
3. "How do no-guess Minesweeper generators work, what board sizes and mine densities stay solvable by logic, and what are the rules and sizes of tentaizu? What are the rules of the 24 game, of JezzBall, and of Pac-Man's ghosts' scatter and chase cycle?"

### Mario Party and the family table (2026-10-11)

The request: another deep dive for game ideas, aimed mainly at parents and older children with brain-game, cozy or nostalgic qualities, starting from the Mario Party games, and treating games a parent and child play **together** as an area worth stretching for the relationship they build, even if those games land in their own area. Everything here is a **proposal**. The rows follow the [earlier passes' columns](#new-candidates-for-parents-and-older-kids); **home** says where a row would live. **†** marks a detail I saw only in a search excerpt and **(recalled)** one from memory. Names in proposals stay generic (no Mario, Bowser or Toad); the research tables name their sources.

**What I found, in five lines.**

1. **The series' draw is its minigames, and co-op is its thin spot.** The first game had 50 to 56 minigames; *Super Mario Party* alone has 84 new ones in seven kinds (30 four-player, 10 two-vs-two, 10 one-vs-three, **10 co-op**, **10 team**, 10 rhythm and 4 that need two consoles), and the *Superstars* page records the lack of co-op minigames and modes as a criticism. A game where everyone is on one side is the series' own gap, not a departure from it.
2. **The island tells a grown-up what to say and rarely gives one something to do.** 70 of the 75 island games carry a grown-up prompt (`coplayHint`), but only three give a second person a *job*: Bounce Back's second paddle, Owl Walk Home's turns, and the green arrow a grown-up taps in Pet Says. The one design guide for this I could open says why that matters: "Interaction often needs to be engineered this way; less structure may fail to elicit dialogic inquiry" ([Takeuchi and Stevens, 2011](https://joanganzcooneycenter.org/wp-content/uploads/2011/12/jgc_coviewing_desktop.pdf), p. 46).
3. **Mario Party itself is for older children than she is.** One parent guide puts its sweet spot at 7 to 9, says reading is required and a full board is 60 to 90 minutes†. What works for her now is the lap-band slice of the same ideas, led by a grown-up; the board and its minigames are a school-age slice. The [bands table](#playing-together-band-by-band) says which is which.
4. **Five shapes carry over, each with a precedent in the series:** a *shared count* (the first game's Hot Rope Jump lets all four win together at 20 jumps), a *split control* (Making Faces: one partner steers left and right, the other forward and back), a *consensus pick* (Match! That! Item!, and Wii Party's Friend Connection), *build one for the other* (Mastermind's maker and breaker) and a *short board where everyone rides one boat* (the shared vehicle).
5. **No claim is made that a game improves a family.** The evidence below is correlational, small or an industry survey; the case is that co-play is common, that one design guide says how to build for it, and that the pediatric advice the notebook already cites is for grown-ups to use media *with* children aged 18 to 24 months ([The lap band](#design-and-learning-references)).

**Added later the same day:** a ranked shortlist of Mario Party minigames for one grown-up to play alone or against bots, with the test that sorts them, in [Mario Party minigames for one](#mario-party-minigames-for-one-alone-and-against-bots); and, after the developer pasted Gemini's answer on the touchscreen minigames, that answer checked page by page in [Touchscreen minigames](#touchscreen-minigames-in-ds-island-tour-and-star-rush-geminis-answer-checked); and Gemini's answer on *Keep Talking* and barrier games, checked, in [Keep Talking and barrier games](#keep-talking-and-barrier-games-geminis-answer-checked); and Gemini's era summary of the team and co-op minigames, checked, in [Team and co-op minigames](#team-and-co-op-minigames-across-the-series-geminis-answer-checked); and Gemini's account of *Wii Party*'s off-screen and pair modes, checked against the pages and the producers' own interview, in [Wii Party's off-screen modes](#wii-partys-off-screen-modes-geminis-answer-checked).

**Access log: what I could and could not read.**
