#### The series, game by game

One row per era; the last column is what it means for this project. The series page says nothing about a design intent for family play beyond reviewers' remarks (IGN called the fifth game a "great game for kids to enjoy with their siblings", and GameSpot said the fourth's accessibility makes it a party "just about anyone can enjoy"). The team's own words on fairness and chance are on the first page of *Iwata Asks: Wii Party*, quoted in [Wii Party's off-screen modes](#wii-partys-off-screen-modes-geminis-answer-checked).

| Game (year, platform) | What it added | What it means here |
| --- | --- | --- |
| *Mario Party* (1998, N64) | A board of dice and stars with a minigame each round; 50 minigames (56 with course variants). **Rotating the stick with a palm blistered hands and wore sticks out**; Nintendo offered free padded gloves†, and rotation minigames were mostly dropped after the second game. Hot Rope Jump had a version where all four win together at 20 jumps | No mashing or spinning. The shared count is the series' own precedent for co-op |
| *Mario Party 2* and *3* (1999, 2000) | Battle, item and duel minigame types (a battle pools coins, the winner taking about 70%); item shops; a story mode with duel boards (3) | Wagers and pots are the theft the house rules decline |
| *Mario Party 4* (2002, GameCube) | The page calls it "the first entry in the series to grant players the ability to team up"; Bowser minigames | Teams are the oldest co-op shape here |
| *Mario Party 5* to *7* (2003 to 2005) | Capsule items and a one-against-one vehicle mode (5); a microphone, a day and night cycle and a Star Bank (6); an eight-player mode and a Bowser event every five turns (7) | A microphone is opt-in on the island, so little to take |
| *Mario Party 8* and *DS* (2007) | Motion remote play and candies (8); a touchscreen and microphone, 73 minigames, and **Download Play for up to four people from one game card** (DS) | Touch is the island's own input. One copy for several players is a barrier worth matching: one iPad, two people |
| *Mario Party 9* (2012, Wii) | **Everyone rides one vehicle**; a captain rolls; scoring in mini stars, where **every minigame ranks everyone and most players earn some, more for a higher place**; bosses beaten together while scoring apart; 78 main minigames | The shared boat, and awards for all. Reviewers fault the luck |
| *Island Tour* (2013, 3DS) | Boards with their own win conditions (Rocket Road: 25 spaces, 10 to 20 minutes, a minigame every third turn); circle-pad rotation returned because the pad can "spin safely" | A board can have a goal other than "most stars", and a short board is in the series' own range |
| *Mario Party 10* (2015, Wii U) | The vehicle returns; **Bowser Party**, where one player on the GamePad is the villain against four; amiibo. The producer calls the roll "100% pure luck" and describes catch-up mechanics† | The asymmetric seat: one person is the weather, not the opponent |
| *Star Rush* (2016, 3DS) | **Everyone moves at once** (Toad Scramble; competitive, with boss fights anyone can join and first place wins†); Balloon Bash puts first and fourth place against second and third in a boss fight; **Rhythm Recital**, the cooperative one: up to four play songs on the touchscreen or A button, and CPU players fill empty slots with no effect | Waiting for a turn is what a small child likes least, and simultaneous play removes it. The CPU-fills-the-seat rule is the pet-takes-the-other-seat rule |
| *Super Mario Party* (2018, Switch) | One Joy-Con each; **84 new minigames in seven kinds**; **Partner Party** (two against two with shared coins, stars, items and dice rolls; both partners roll at once and move the sum†), **River Survival** (four paddle one raft downriver "before time runs out"; paddling on a side turns the raft away from it†), Sound Stage (rhythm), a one-player Challenge Road of 80 minigames, and **practice built into the rules screen**; settings for CPU number and difficulty and for 10, 15 or 20 turns | The richest source: simultaneous dice, split steering, practice before play |
| *Mario Party Superstars* (2021) | 100 returning minigames and five N64 boards; Mt. Minigames (score attack for sports and puzzles, coin battle, tag match, trio challenge); turns up to 30; **an on-screen warning on the palm-spinning minigame**; the page criticizes the lack of co-op minigames and modes | Co-op is thin even in the best-loved set |
| *Super Mario Party Jamboree* (2024; Switch 2 edition 2025) | 112 minigames, seven boards; **Motion Island**, three cooperative motion modes, among them **Rhythm Kitchen** (a cooperative cooking rhythm rated by three judges, four difficulties) and a cooperative ball-guiding mode of 30 levels for up to four; **Kaboom Squad**, eight against an impostor, where **defeated players are trapped in bubbles and teammates revive them**; a one-player Party-Planner Trek (free roaming, requests, a boss after 30 mini stars, up to three allies); **Pro Rules** (12 turns, a vote on minigames, minigame help and motion minigames can be switched off); the Switch 2 edition adds mouse, camera and microphone minigames | Revive-by-a-friend is the gentle form of a mistake; help is a setting; a solo mode has allies |
| *Wii Party* (2010, NDcube; not a Mario Party game) | **Pair Games**, "cooperative or competitive": Friend Connection (partners answer five questions and do better if their answers match), Balance Boat (partners balance twenty characters on a ship's sails; a computer can be the partner); **House Party**, played away from the screen with the remotes as objects (Hide and Hunt: one player hides one to four remotes while the others search, with a sound every ten seconds; one remote passed around like a bomb; one game with a real deck of cards); Buddy Quiz (others predict a chosen buddy's answers) | Matching minds, a hider, and play that leaves the screen: the model for this pass |
| *Wii Party U* (2013, Wii U, NDcube) | Four modes: TV Party boards, **House Party** (eight group games that need the GamePad and Wii Remotes: Button Smashers, Water Runners, Do U Know Mii?, Lost-and-Found Square, Sketchy Situation, Name That Face, Feed Mii!, Dance With Mii; five need three or four players), **GamePad Party** (tabletop games for one or two, among them Animal Matchup, a picture-matching game for two) and Minigame Mode (among them Bridge Burners, two to four in co-op, per the Family Gaming Database). No Pair or Buddy mode | The second screen put to work: one person sees the place and the rest must find it. Nintendo World Report: "all of the House Party games are awesome" |
| *1-2-Switch* (2017) | Its producer called it an "icebreaker for all kinds of fun communication"; players look at each other, not the screen† | The reason for off-screen suggestions and for talk as part of a game |

#### What the minigames are made of

The series page does not give one official list; it names **four-player** games in three kinds (cooperative, where everyone wins or loses together; competitive free-for-all; and non-competitive free-for-all), **two-against-two**, **one-against-three**, single-player, **battle**, **item** and **duel** (all three from the second game), Bowser minigames (from the fourth) and boss battles (the page cites two per board in the ninth). *Superstars* lists 53 four-player, 18 one-against-three, 18 two-against-two, 5 duel, 5 item and 6 sports-and-puzzles games. The coin minigames are described as having "no winner or loser". Opened examples:

| Minigame (kind) | What it is, from its page | The shape that carries over |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Net Worth (co-op) | All players hold the corners of a net and raise it at the same time to catch fish; 30 seconds | A **unison action**: everyone has to move together |
| Sort of Fun (co-op) | Players pass sports balls around and drop each in its matching basket; a mistake costs a point; timed | **Hand-off and sorting**; the clock and the point loss are what the house rules decline |
| Fetch Quest (co-op) | In a cave, bring nine diamonds to the centre; some can only be reached after someone stands on a switch; players can pass diamonds | **Complementary jobs**: one holds the switch, one carries |
| Suit Yourselves (co-op) | The host shows suited cards, flips them, swaps at least one and may rotate the deck; each teammate watches one card; four right wins, four wrong loses | A **memory task split by card**: each person owns one |
| Go with the Flow (co-op) | Each player spins their own fan to steer balloons and pop as many as they can | Everyone's own control feeding one result (the spinning is not copied) |
| Making Faces (two against two) | Teammates rebuild a face by stepping on buttons to drop three features; one partner controls left and right, the other forward and back | **Split axes**: together they can do what neither can alone |
| Maths of Glory (two against two) | Each team stops slot reels showing 1 to 6; the numbers multiply to fire cannonballs at the rival castle | Stopping reels to make a **product** (the castle is out) |
| Match! That! Item! (Kaboom Squad, eight players) | Every player stops a spinner on the same picture out of six; the group scores a point per match and two more if all eight match; 60 seconds | **Consensus**: matching minds, scored together |
| Hot Rope Jump (four-player) | Jump a swinging rope of lava bubbles, holding the button longer for a higher jump; in the first game all four can win by reaching 20 jumps, and in the second and in *Superstars* the last one standing wins | A **shared count** (kept) and **elimination** (dropped) in one game |
| Shy Guy Says (four-player) | Copy the white or red flag the host raises; a wrong flag is out | Simon Says, which Pet Says already is |
| Treasure Divers (coin) | Dive for chests and bring them to the boat; "no winner or loser" | Collecting together with no ranking |
| Bumper Balls (four-player) | Ride a ball and knock rivals off the platform | Pushing others off: out |

#### What parents and the research say

- **Reading and length.** One parent guide says the instructions flash by and reading is required, so "playing together" means "you're going to be very involved"; it puts the sweet spot at 7 to 9 and a full board at 60 to 90 minutes†. Common Sense Media rates *Jamboree* 6+† and the ESRB gives it Everyone with mild fantasy violence†.
- **Luck as the leveler.** The same guide says dice and minigames let a six-year-old beat a ten-year-old and a parent†. The *Mario Party 10* producer: the roll is "100% pure luck", and catch-up mechanics keep the end open†. Reviewers fault the series' reliance on luck (the series page, opened). The team itself, on the first page of *Iwata Asks: Wii Party* (opened): "There is an element of chance, so one good player won't win all the time."
- **Easier ways in.** A minigame mode lets a young child replay simpler games, and pairing a grown-up with a child shares the difficulty†. For young children elsewhere, guides name assist modes as the gold standard: a helper that fishes a player out of a pit (*Kirby's Return to Dream Land Deluxe*), smart steering (*Mario Kart 8 Deluxe*), a bubble that holds an overwhelmed child safe (*Super Mario Bros. Wonder*), a second player who points the way (*Super Mario Galaxy*); give the child the easier role; and the quickest way to lose a child's interest is to take the controller when they struggle†.
- **Coyne et al. 2011** (*Journal of Adolescent Health*; 287 families; adolescents; correlational): co-playing was linked to less internalizing and aggressive behavior and more prosocial behavior for **girls only**, and marginally to connectedness; a mature-rated game weakened the link; no significant effect for boys†. It says nothing about a one-year-old.
- **ESA surveys:** about three quarters of American parents play weekly, and in 2026 81% of parents said they play with their children, 52% weekly†. An industry survey: it counts appetite, not benefit.
- **Munzer et al. 2019** (*Pediatrics*; 37 parent and toddler pairs in one lab): with e-books the pairs talked less about the story and parents spent more of their words on the device, such as telling the child not to push buttons†. This is the warning for this pass: **a row that makes the grown-up say "don't touch that" has failed.**

#### The screens: what the series does that the rules decline, and what the design guide asks

| The series does | The rules decline it because | What survives |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Stealing stars or coins, red and villain spaces | Things she earned are never taken | Co-op team scoring (Net Worth, Sort of Fun, Fetch Quest); awards every seat gets (the ninth game's own rule: everyone is ranked and most earn mini stars) |
| Last-one-standing elimination (Hot Rope Jump in later games, Shy Guy Says, Bumper Balls) | No one is out | The first game's Hot Rope Jump: all win at 20 jumps |
| Mashing and stick-spinning | Blisters, and an adult's strength beats a child's | Taps timed to a beat; one-finger drags |
| Clocks, including co-op ones (Net Worth's 30 seconds, River Survival's timer) | No round ends on a timer | Counted goals; "until the dock" |
| A 60 to 90 minute board | Comfortable stopping | A board of six to twelve spaces that stops anywhere and resumes |
| Instructions you must read | Navigation never requires reading | Spoken rules, the ghost finger, and practice before play (the series' own rules-screen practice) |
| Luck as the leveler | It makes a loss feel arbitrary | Kept only as a soft leveler on the board's dice (a die that only moves you forward); **level-matched rounds** for the minigames |
| Motion controls, microphone, camera | Opt-in only | The same; nothing here needs them |

| The design guide's principle (pp. 44 to 51; its quotes are exact) | What it asks of a row |
| --- | --- |
| **Kid-driven**: "Build tools and experiences that revolve around a child's existing interests" | The child initiates: chooses the role, the picture, the stop |
| **Multiple planes of engagement**: media "designed for the lowest common denominator" "merely patronizes and alienates" | A grown-up has a puzzle, a par or a joke of their own while a toddler plays the same screen |
| **Differentiation of roles**: tasks "match up to individual maturity"; "force them to talk to coordinate"; roles also "motivate individuals to try their best and not let their partners down" | Each seat has a different job; the talk is part of the game |
| **Scaffolds to scaffold**: guidance "in ways that don't require a lot of prior prep"; explicit scaffolds "can turn the situation into something perceived as pedagogical"; "Subtler cues will suffice" | The pet *asks* the grown-up a question in character (the report's case: Elmo models dialogic reading for the adult) instead of a card the grown-up must read |
| **Co-creation**: "Consuming content together entails little interaction, but creating ... requires quite a bit of dialog and coordination" | A row ends in something made together |
| **Fit**: it "should easily slot into existing routines" | Short, restartable, no setup |
| **Focus on content, not control** (p. 44): "Don't touch!" and "jockeying for controllers" "kept to a minimum" | Separate lanes and no shared targets; the pet announces whose turn it is |
| Factors of joint media engagement (pp. 50 and 51): "Number of access points to control screen", "Face-to-face vs. side-by-side", "Level of conflict over control", "Legitimate peripheral participation" | Side by side; one lane each; a grown-up who only watches still takes part |

#### The seat contract

Every two-seat row answers these once; a row names only where it differs. A **seat** is a person at the screen.

- **Seats.** Seat 1 is the active profile. Seat 2 is anonymous (Bounce Back's grown-up paddle today) until Game night puts profiles in seats ([Lands](ROADMAP.md#lands-navigation-and-discovery) stage E).
- **Adaptation.** Only seat 1's role feeds `difficulty.ts`; the grown-up's role is scaffolding and is never measured. A round in which seat 1 plays the *other* role, or the grown-up does seat 1's task, counts as supported completion, not evidence of mastery (the [AGENTS.md](../AGENTS.md) rule). This is a decision (below).
- **Credit.** The sticker, journal entries and a kept creation go to the active profile.
- **Solo fallback.** The pet takes the other seat, as in Bounce Back and Owl Walk Home, so every row can be finished alone, and a hidden-information row is a machine-checked puzzle.
- **Two touches.** Each seat has its own lane; targets in each are at least 100 units; deliberate multi-touch uses Bounce Back's pointer-to-side map and `palmOnGlass()`, with the cancellation and leave-the-screen cleanup the rules already require.
- **Demo.** The how-to card's ghost finger is one finger; a two-role demonstration needs the card to drive two, with the pet as the second. That is an infrastructure gap that arrives with the first two-seat game.
- **Voice.** Lines address two people: `{name}` for her and role words ("the turner") for the other seat; a `{partner}` placeholder would be a code change.
- **Face to face.** Playing with the iPad flat between two people (the 1-2-Switch idea) means laying out half the screen upside down, and `view.ts` fits one design area. That is a real layout cost, so every row is side by side, and the table layout is a decision.

#### Playing together, band by band

| Band | What playing together is | Already there | This pass |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Lap** (her, now) | The grown-up leads; she taps, listens and watches the pet; a lap, a song, a clap | Bounce Back's play-together level, Pet Says (off screen), the grown-up prompts | Row Together's lap level and Skip Rope's pet-jumps level, the only rows she can use soon, both led by a grown-up; Heave-Ho |
| **Toddler** | Parallel play, imitation, turns with a grown-up and the pet | Owl Walk Home's turns | Row Together; Two-Knob Doodle's scribble; a grown-up hides and she finds |
| **Preschool and pre-K** | Roles and rules: "your job, my job"; hiding games | | Same Pick; Make One for Me (hide, bury, choose the stones); the party board's mini board with one die |
| **School** (6 to 8) | The series' own sweet spot (7 to 9†): dice, a board, minigames at her level | Couch trips, for grown-ups | The full board; Mystery Friend; Tilt Together; the Manual from about 8 |
| **9 to 11 and grown-up** | A brain duo: a par to chase, a puzzle one builds for the other, hidden information | The couch puzzles | Make One for Me at higher levels; Tilt Together with a par; the Manual; Game night |

#### How the research lands on rows already here

No second row for the same game. [Buddy Delivery](#candidates), Complementary jobs, [Shape Buddies](#candidates), Two Friends, Echo Friends, Pond Charades, Pond Bingo, Island Race and Snap Pairs are older co-play rows; this pass adds the contract above and the design screen they all pass through, and does not re-propose them.

| Source | Existing row | What it adds |
| --- | --- | --- |
| River Survival | **Raft Delivery** (couch) | The sourced rule: paddling on a side turns the raft away from it. The clock and the minigames that earn time are dropped. Touch-first and for every band: **Row Together** |
| The shared vehicle, Partner Party's shared dice, mini stars for all | **Island Toy Fair** (the overlaps table says "revisit only for the child's island"), couch trips | This pass is the revisit: the **Island Party Board** |
| Shy Guy Says; Musical Statues and Magic Xylophone (Bluey†) | **Pet Says** | Nothing new: it already holds "Pet says" and freeze dance |
| Suit Yourselves | Peekaboo Barn's planned Switcheroo levels; Memory Match | A memory task split between two people, one card each: a two-seat mode below |
| Rhythm Kitchen, Sound Stage, Rhythm Recital | **Rhythm Neighbors**, Beat Builder | Nothing new |
| Bowser Party's one seat as the weather; Bluey's Keepy Uppy†, where the father's fans make the balloon harder | **Keepy Uppy**, Bounce Back | The **breeze seat** seed |
| Twenty questions; Guess Who? (**recalled**) | **Who Is It?** (board games table) | **Mystery Friend**, below this table |
| Wii Party's Friend Connection and Buddy Quiz; Match! That! Item! | None | **Same Pick** |
| Mastermind's maker and breaker | **Secret Code** | A mode: seat 1 sets the code, the machine marks each guess, seat 2 breaks it, then swap (**Make One for Me**) |
| Wii Party's Hide and Hunt | Peekaboo Barn, Photo Safari | The hider is a person: the **Chime Hunt** seed, and **Count and Hide**, where the screen counts |
| Wii Party's Time Bomb and Word Bomb; Pass the Parcel (Bluey†) | **Pet Says** (its freeze dance) | **Pass the Pet**, for a circle of three or more |
| Wii Party U's Lost-and-Found Square | Peekaround Island (position words) | **Where Am I?**, a variant of Mission Cards |
| Wii Party's Balance Boat | Block Tower | A two-seat mode, in the table of two-seat modes |

**Mystery Friend (Who Is It? as twenty questions).** Mosher and Hornsby's 1966 study of twenty questions separates *hypothesis-scanning* questions, one guess ("Is it the dog?"), from *constraint-seeking* ones that test what several candidates share ("Does it have four legs?"). Later papers say younger children overwhelmingly ask the first kind and the second grows between about 6 and 11, but that children as young as 4 ask mostly effective splitting questions when the procedure offers them, and that training results are mixed†. So the ladder is: the grown-up asks and the board shows the friends that remain, shrinking; then she picks from a few picture questions (hat? bow? blue?); then she picks the question that splits the group best, and the hint lights it. Rule checks: the picture questions can single out every friend on every generated board, and the "best" question is the one whose split is closest to half (a test that no other question is more balanced). Picture friends from `critter.ts` and `props.ts`; no reading. Effort S–M · island pre-K to school.

#### Candidates for two

| Idea (after) | Play and skill | Smallest useful version, mistakes and rule checks | Effort · home |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Row Together** (River Survival and Raft Delivery; "Row, Row, Row Your Boat", traditional) | *Fun action:* two people each tap or stroke their own side of a big raft; a stroke on the left turns the raft right, so going straight means rowing together, and the pair carries a picnic across a river. *Skill:* left and right, tempo and taking part together; later route choice and counting | **Lap:** any tap rows both sides and the raft moves to a rowing tune. ("Row, Row, Row Your Boat" uses the fourth note the pentatonic rule leaves out, so the traditional tune waits on the roadmap's [pentatonic exception](WAITING-ON-DEVELOPER.md#waiting-on-the-developer); a pentatonic chant of the same shape stands in.) **Toddler:** a big paddle for each side, and the river carries the raft to a dock that is always in view. **Preschool and pre-K:** steer round lily pads; a fork where a picture clue says which way (Mail Carrier's map reading). **School:** carry a counted load, plan a loop. **No clock**: River Survival's timer and its time-earning minigames are dropped. Island Tour's Paddle Skedaddle paddles a canoe with the stylus (its steering rule is not on its page). A bump on the bank turns the raft and plays a boing; nothing is lost. Rules in a pure fixed-step function a test reads: left and right strokes turn symmetrically, every stroke pattern reaches the dock (the current guarantees it), and the dock is reachable from every fork. Solo: the pet rows the other side, slowly and steadily. Seat 1's strokes are what difficulty reads | M · island lap to school; the couch's Raft Delivery reuses the same function |
| **Two-Knob Doodle** (Etch A Sketch, 1960: the left knob moves the stylus across and the right up and down, and "turning both knobs simultaneously makes diagonal lines"; Making Faces' split; Soil Toil, where each partner spins one wheel with the stylus and the car goes toward the slower wheel) | *Fun action:* each person owns one slider, one for left and right along the bottom and one for up and down along the side; together they move one pen and draw. *Skill:* x and y, coordination, and talking ("up a bit!") | Free doodle first (nothing is wrong; a big wipe button clears it, not a shake); then follow a dotted star or house through gates, where any line inside the corridor counts; then "draw a circle together", shown by how close it came and never as a score (hard and funny). A finished picture can be kept as "our picture" (needs the creation-kind decision). Rules: the pen is a function of the two slider positions; a test that a scripted two-axis path stays inside each shape's corridor. Solo: two thumbs, or the pet glides the vertical slider along the shape in the first levels | S–M · island toddler (scribble) to school; grown-up cozy |
| **Tilt Together** (BRIO's Labyrinth, first made in Sweden in 1946: a maze on two axles, each turned by a knob, with a marble to guide round holes; the 2024 game's cooperative ball-guiding mode, known to me only from a description) | *Fun action:* two knobs tilt a board so a marble rolls to a star; one person owns left and right, the other front and back. *Skill:* tilt as cause and effect, anticipating inertia, planning a route | A sloped board with walls; the marble rolls toward the low side; holes are soft puddles that put it back with a plop (a bump, not a loss). **A first form needs no new physics:** Penguin Slide with split directions (below), whose solver already gives par and hints. The continuous form needs a top-down rolling model with friction and walls, which `ball.ts` does not have and the Mini Putt and Roll Table rows also need, so build it once. Rules: fixed steps so a course replays; a breadth-first search over a discretized board proves every board solvable and gives its par | M–L · island school (7 to 11) and grown-up courses |
| **Same Pick** (Match! That! Item!; Wii Party's Friend Connection and Buddy Quiz) | *Fun action:* two people each secretly choose one of six pictures (or answer a picture question such as "something you'd take to the beach") and reveal together; a match makes both pets dance and a miss makes them shrug and go again. *Skill:* guessing what another mind will choose, categories, talk | The pet counts to three while the other seat shuts their eyes (an honor system, the same one Make One for Me and Chime Hunt lean on; no built game has a human hider yet), and the two picks stay covered until the reveal. A round is five picks, **a mismatch is never a miss**, matches fill a lantern, and the round ends after five whatever the count (one sticker). Ladder: favorites ("which do you like?"), a category ("something round"), "pick what *your partner* will pick", then eight pictures with fewer obvious fits. Rule checks: every prompt has at least two and at most four fitting pictures among the six, with none repeated in a round (a test over the prompt deck). Solo: the pet picks with a seeded taste ("likes round things"), and she learns to predict the pet | S–M · island pre-K to school and a grown-up window |
| **Make One for Me** (Mastermind, 1970: one codemaker and one codebreaker who swap each game; Excitebike's design mode and Lode Runner's editor, already in the Track Maker row; Hide and Hunt) | *Fun action:* one person builds a puzzle in a game she already knows and the other solves it, then they swap. *Skill:* seeing a puzzle from the maker's side; constraints; for the maker, the pleasure of a trick | **Secret Code first:** seat 1 chooses the stones for a door's slots while seat 2 looks away; the machine marks every guess, so the maker needs no skill; then swap. Then **Robot Path** (place rocks and a star; the solver says "this one needs 7 steps"), **Treasure Map** (bury the treasure and read the clues), **Peekaboo Barn** (hide the animal) and **Critter Crossing** (choose the rule). **The machine validates what is made**, so any creation is playable and shows its own par ("tricky!"); a board with no solution is refused kindly. Nothing made is kept unless a creation kind is added. Rule checks: every makeable board is solvable, and the shown par equals a brute-force search | S per game · island (a mode of each) and grown-up |
| **Island Party Board** (the shared vehicle; Partner Party's shared dice; mini stars for every player; the revisit of Island Toy Fair) | *Fun action:* a short path of about a dozen spaces that two people ride in one boat. Both roll a die at once, the pet hops the **sum**, and a space starts a minigame, a fork, a lantern or a friendly visit. *Skill:* adding to 12 (count on from the bigger die), planning at a fork, and each stop's own learning | **Smallest: a mini board with no minigames** (the Island Race row plus a second die) whose spaces only cheer. Then three-card minigame stops where each seat plays the game at **its own level** (`go.game` already plays a level without moving the adaptive one, as picnic steps do; the couch's `level(stop)` becomes per seat). **No stealing, no red spaces, no elimination.** The captain seat rotates and chooses at forks. At the end each seat gets a **ribbon**, the bonus-star idea: a different silly one chosen from what each did (steady paws: fewest misses; brave hopper: played the hardest level; curious: tried the most different games), with "good friend" as the fallback so nobody gets none. Ribbons are words and a picture on the finale, never stickers and never possessions; a test that with two seats the ribbons are always two different ones. Rule checks: the sum's distribution, every path reaches the end whatever is rolled, forks rejoin, and any space can be resumed. About ten minutes, stoppable anywhere (Island Tour's 25-space Rocket Road takes 10 to 20). Targets Game night or the map, never new couch-shell features (decided 2026-10-09) | S (mini board), L (full) · school; as Game night, grown-up |
| **Skip Rope** (Hot Rope Jump's cooperative original; playground jump rope, with its chants) | *Fun action:* one seat turns a rope and one jumps; the pair counts up together to 20, then by fives to 100, and a chant plays. *Skill:* rhythm, the counting sequence, skip counting; the turner's steady beat | The pet turns the rope and she taps to jump (lap: the pet jumps whenever she taps anywhere); then a grown-up turns (a steady tap on the beat) while she jumps; then they swap. **No tripping:** a late jump just doesn't add to the count and the rope keeps swinging. The numeral is shown and spoken; traditional rhymes only, each reviewed. Rule checks: the jump window is a pure function (a test), the count never goes down, and any tap rhythm eventually reaches 20. Timing windows are playtest values | S–M · island toddler to school |
| **Lantern Keeper's Manual** (*Keep Talking and Nobody Explodes*, opened: one player sees the bomb and the others read the manual; the same shape as the "barrier games" used in speech and language teaching†) | *Fun action:* one person sees a music box with dials, gems and buttons and describes it; the other has the manual that says what to turn for each pattern; a right turn plays a note and the box sings a tune. *Skill:* precise description, listening, reading rules; the roles swap | One module (colored gems, which button), the manual a plain page generated from the same seed in the grown-ups' page, printable or on a second screen. No bomb and no clock; a wrong press is a soft sour note and the box says "again". Rule checks: every module state has exactly one right action under its manual, and a gadget and its manual agree for a thousand seeds (a test). **Needs a decision**: hidden information cannot be kept on one iPad, so it needs a printed page or a second screen (*Keep Talking* itself keeps its manual off the screen; [Mission Cards](#keep-talking-and-barrier-games-geminis-answer-checked) shows a physical barrier does the same job, and is the cheap first half) | L · island school (8 to 11) and a grown-up window |

#### Seeds and two-seat modes

| Seed | First complete loop | Notes |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Heave-Ho** (Tug o' War, the first game's; its palm-spinning is dropped) | Both seats pull a friendly bear on the beat of a chant; a pull counts only on the beat, and taps between beats do nothing, so there is nothing to mash; the bear slides into the pond with a splash and gets a hug | No clock. Taps, never spinning. A timing mode in the family of Bounce Back |
| **Breeze seat** (Bowser Party's asymmetric seat; Bluey's Keepy Uppy†) | One seat keeps a balloon up; the other is the gentle wind who may puff it sideways; it never pops | The child as the wind is the joke. A mode of Keepy Uppy or Bounce Back |
| **Parachute Pals** (Net Worth's unison lift) | Two people each press a corner of a blanket and slide up together on "ready, set, lift" to toss a pompom | The lift must be within a margin; lap: the pet holds the other corner. A margin is a playtest value |
| **Chime Hunt** (Wii Party's Hide and Hunt) | The grown-up hides the iPad and it chimes an animal's voice every ten seconds, as the original's remotes do; she follows the sound | Needs the screen kept awake and a decision about hiding a device. The original scores the hider by the seekers' seconds; the island drops that. An off-screen game, never required |
| **Dream Cards** (Dixit; [publisher mechanics opened 2026-10-14](https://www.libellud.com/game/dixit/), upgrading the recalled seed) | One person chooses an original dream picture and pictured/spoken clue; the other points, then reveal and compare associations. School–parent first; optional swap, all interpretations unscored | [Definition packet](deep-dives/subjects/social-emotional.md#dream-cards-turn-the-existing-seed-into-a-definition), [roadmap](deep-dives/subjects/RECOMMENDATIONS.md#social-emotional-play-recommendations). Three pictures/three clue tokens, one reveal, shared-device privacy and solo pet fallback to define; no speech recognition or copied Dixit art. Neighbor: Pond/Animal Charades |
| **Two-Leg Walk** (Dungeon Dash; the three-legged race) | Each seat owns one leg of a friendly walker; a step needs the two legs to alternate on the beat of a chant, and the walker crosses stepping stones to a picnic | No clock; two taps on the same leg make it wobble and wait, never fall. Lap: the pet taps the second leg. Row Together's rule in legs |
| **Count and Hide** (Hide 'n' Hunt; hide-and-seek) | The pet counts out loud to ten (later by fives to a hundred) while everyone hides, then calls "ready or not!"; a big Found button ends the round. The screen is the counter and the referee, not the game; the iPad can stay hidden and chime an animal every ten seconds (Chime Hunt) | No timer ends anything: the hunt ends when she is found, with a sticker. Lap: the grown-up hides in plain sight. Needs only voice lines for the numbers |
| **Pass the Pet** (Time Bomb and Word Bomb without the bomb; Pass the Parcel, **recalled**) | A tune plays from the iPad on the table while a plush pet goes round a circle; when it stops, whoever holds the pet names one thing from the card (something red) or gives the next "Pet says" move. Nobody is out | Pet Says' freeze-dance music stop for three or more people; the screen stays on the table, not in anyone's hands. Bluey's version is unchecked (prompt 7) |

**Two-seat modes of games already built** (S each; the role split is the point, and none needs a second profile):

| Game | The two roles | What it adds |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Penguin Slide, Ferry Jam | One owns left and right (the horizontal boats), the other up and down (the vertical ones) | Making Faces' split; the existing solvers give par and hints, and the couch's seeded twin boards show the pattern |
| Secret Code | Maker and breaker | Make One for Me |
| Pattern Train | Take turns adding a car; "continue my pattern" | Co-creation of a pattern |
| Market Stall, Lemonade Stand | Shopkeeper and customer swap; she is the expert, and a task card tells the grown-up to pay a shell short, a mistake she can catch | Teaching the grown-up (the teachable-agent idea, recalled) |
| Pet Kitchen | One cuts and one serves; the pieces must be fair | Fractions as sharing between two people |
| Rainbow Fingers | Two painters, each with a color; where strokes cross the colors mix (red, yellow and blue, as the game says) | "Our picture" |
| Garden Grow | One plants and the other is the weather (rain, sun); its [deep dive](deep-dives/garden-grow.md) names the seat in the co-play tip | The asymmetric seat |
| Memory Match, Peekaboo Barn | Each seat owns half the cards | Suit Yourselves' split |
| Lasso Loops | Each seat draws a loop; a friend is caught only when both loops close round it | Joint attention: the two have to look at the same one (Etch 'n' Catch) |
| Block Tower | Take turns adding one block to one tower, saying first whether it will stand (an unscored prediction); a topple is rebuilt together | Balance Boat's shared ship, with the `topples` rule as judge and the pet as the partner when she plays alone |
| *Built:* Bounce Back, Owl Walk Home | A second paddle; turns with the pet | The two precedents the contract is drawn from |

**Set aside from this pass, with the reason:** every minigame that ends in someone being knocked out or off (Bumper Balls, last-one-standing Hot Rope Jump, Shy Guy Says' elimination), stealing and pot-splitting, the rotating-stick and mashing minigames, motion, microphone and camera minigames (opt-in only, and none is needed), online matchmaking and a 20-player race (no account or server), and Koopathlon's setbacks of 10 to 40 spaces.

#### Mario Party minigames for one: alone and against bots

*Added later the same day, after the developer asked which minigames the developer's wife could play alone, with or without bots.* Every row below was checked on its own wiki page (summarized by the fetch tool; **†** as above). The first list I used paraphrased some games wrongly (Mushroom Mix-Up is a last-one-standing platform game, not a pattern game), so no row rests on that list alone.

**The test.** A minigame is a good candidate for one grown-up, alone or against bots, when it:

1. gives each player their own lane, board or count, so a bot's run can be computed without watching the player's (a bot leaks nothing);
2. needs one stick or one button (the grown-up route is played on a TV with a controller);
3. ends on a count or a finish line, not a clock;
4. scores exactly (hits, distance, percent, right answers), which the couch's "personal best and par" template wants;
5. has nobody touching anybody: no knocking off, stunning, shared plates or contested targets.

**What Mario Party already does for one player** (opened). *Superstars*' Mt. Minigames has Score Attack (the puzzle games: three minutes, or a point target of 100 to 1000), Endless mode (Hot Rope Jump, Booksquirm and Ticktock Hop, ending at 999 pages or 99 jumps), a Free Play "High-Score" set (a fan site lists 17†), Coin Battle (5, 10 or 15 rounds, coins by place: 10, 3, 2, 0) and Trio Challenge (stages of five minigames against CPUs rated Easy to Master; a loss restarts the stage). *Super Mario Party* has Challenge Road (80 minigames, each with a goal) and Mariothon, a five-minigame cup. Nintendo has already sorted what works alone; this shortlist agrees where they overlap.

**The shortlist, ranked** by how little must change and how well a bot can play it. Names are generic; the source is in the first column. Rows 1 to 4 pass all five checks as their pages give them (Dinger Derby's page does not say in words that each player bats on their own; it gives each a fixed 30 pitches).

| Idea (after) | From its page | Alone, and against bots | Change, and where it already lives |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **1. Batting Cage** (Dinger Derby) | Hit as many of **30 pitches** as you can; A swings; a fixed count, not a clock; most hits wins | Score is hits of 30, so the exact best is 30; a bot is the same timing function with seeded error | None needed. A new one-button timing game (nearest: Beat Hopper's timing windows). S |
| **2. Stop the Cart** (Night-Light Fright) | Shine a light to stop a charging Chain Chomp as close to you as possible; A only; each player has their own Chomp, 30.0 units away; a hit scores no distance | Five rounds; score is distance; very tense against three bots because each round lasts seconds | A bump scores zero for that round and play goes on. A new one-button game. S |
| **3. Crowd Count** (Goomba Spotting; Senseless Census as its second level) | Tally the Goombas running across the screen, A for each, separate counts, closest to the true total wins; Buzzy Beetles are mixed in. Census: count Toads wandering a train car among lookalike items | Score is how far off (0 is exact); bots miscount at a seeded rate that grows with the crowd | The stream is seeded so the total is known (a test: it equals the targets). Census's 20-second clock becomes a "lock in" button. Brain Break's "count a flash of critters". S |
| **4. Crayon Trace** (Trace Race) | Each player moves a giant crayon along their own winding guideline; stick only; "not racing-based"; scored by the percentage of adherence | Score is percent; a bot wobbles by seeded noise around the exact path; cozy | None needed. A grown-up cousin of Letter Trails' tracing. S |
| **5. Puddle Path** (Trip Navigator) | Each player races through their own area littered with banana peels to the goal in the centre; stick only; a slip stuns for one second | One course per player from the same seed; finish time or place; bots follow a planner | Finish time as a main score is the pending [racing-time decision](WAITING-ON-DEVELOPER.md#waiting-on-the-developer). Mossy Path, Munch Maze. S–M |
| **6. Toy Car Derby** (Slot-Car Derby; Later Skater) | The stick's tilt speeds the car, releasing slows it; four laps; fast corners spin out; holding at "Go!" costs seconds; a lapped player is rammed out. Skater: steer a rink with the stick, hold A, five laps | A time trial against a ghost and bots in their own slots; par from an optimal-throttle search over the track | Drop the ramming; the finish ends it. Waits on the racing-time decision (the Time Trial with a ghost row). M |
| **7. Photo Match** (Camera-Ready) | Each player holds their own camera in a plaza and matches the photo shown each round; stick moves, two buttons zoom, one shoots; closer scores more; three rounds, 30 s | Score is closeness, and the exact best frame is known; bots frame with seeded error; cozy | The 30-second limit becomes a confirm. The grown-up form of Photo Safari. M (art) |
| **8. Sharp Eyes** (Thwomp the Difference; Absent Minded; Big-Top Quiz) | Spot the one picture that differs among four, near-identical from round 3 (five rounds); spot the one character missing from a group; study images on rolling balls, then answer questions about them | Score is right answers; a bot answers right with a seeded chance that falls as pictures grow alike; the generator guarantees exactly one answer (a test) | The 5, 3, 2, 1 order bonus is the one place a bot makes a clock (below). Photo Hunt, What Changed?, Hidden Pond. S–M |

**Also good, and already in this notebook:** Hot Rope Jump, whose first-game rule has all four win together at 20 jumps, is [Skip Rope](#candidates-for-two), where the bots can be teammates; Chip-Shot Challenge (aim and power, closest to the hole, one shot) and Stone-Eye Bowling (steer one boulder, one throw) are the Mini Putt and Roll Table rows; Cast Aways (set a cast distance, fish for coins worth 1, 5 and 10) is Fishing Pond; Messy Memory (put scattered items back, then close the curtain) is What Changed?.

**Avoid, with the reason** (each seen on its page unless marked).

- *Knocking, stunning, last one standing:* Bumper Balls, Mushroom Mix-Up, Dizzy Dancing and All the Marbles (punches), Sandwiched (stomping).
- *A shared plate, cloud or seesaw (a bot would need real AI):* Can Take Pancake (the page implies one plate), Storm Chasers, Treetop Treasure, Hot Cross Blocks (picks that collide).
- *Mashing:* Rockin' Raceway (rapid alternating presses), Treasure Divers, Tug o' War (the palm-injury game, from the series page).
- *Luck:* Follow the Money (random enemies), Timing Is Money (a roulette).
- *Motion, rumble or a wheel:* Night Lights, Fast Fishing, Metal Detectors, Precision Gardening, Sizzling Stakes. Tilt-a-Golf and Twist and Sort could lose the motion by mapping tilt to a stick axis.
- *Falling-block puzzles* (Mario's Puzzle Party, Stick and Spin): Score Attack exists, but in the versus form clears send blocks to the opponent. A genre decision (the set-aside Tetris note; the Gem Swap and Block Garden rows) and a bot for them is real AI.
- *Not opened:* the disambiguation pages (Roll Call, Block Star, Coney Island, Rumble Fishing), Mecha Marathon, and every four-player game not named here (Gemini prompt 8).

**Against bots: what it takes.**

- **Everyone on one board at one moment.** A bot leaks nothing, so for the independent rows the couch's `twin` rule (each player on their own board in turn, so nobody faces a board whose answer they watched) is not needed: one seeded board, the player and the bots together, ranked at the end.
- **A bot is a ghost.** The how-to demo already runs a real, separate instance of a game on a seed through `autoplay`; an opponent is that instance with a seeded skill setting, run beside the player in a small lane or ahead of time, and compared by `score`. Nintendo's own tiers run Easy to Master.
- **Today's bots play perfectly.** `autoplay` is "the input a capable player would press", and the puzzle bots (Penguin Slide, Lantern Lights, Ferry Jam, Pond Conga) follow a solver or a hint and never err, so a bot opponent needs a skill dial that makes it imperfect: a seeded reaction delay, miss rate or aim error.
- **Scores by place.** The series scores by place (5, 3, 2, 1 by order of correct answers; 10, 3, 2, 0 coins in Coin Battle), and a five-game cup is the Mariothon shape. When the bots are faster, "first correct gets 5" acts as a clock; it is relative to the bots, not an absolute limit, and the personal-best score stays exact. Say so on the how-to screen.
- **Endless is lives in disguise.** Superstars' Endless ends when you are hit; here a hit is a counted bump and the run goes on to a stated count (99 jumps, a fixed number of pages).

**Decisions this needs from the developer.** (Also under [Waiting on the developer](WAITING-ON-DEVELOPER.md#waiting-on-the-developer).)

1. **Where bots live.** The fastest build is a seeded bot in the second seat of the existing face-off games, but the 2026-10-09 decision says new grown-up features go on the map and Game night, not into the couch shell, and a bot seat in the couch shell now runs against it. Lean: build the five independent games' rules and bots as pure modules now (they are the same either way) and let Game night host the cups.
2. **May a bot be named a cup's winner?** The finale names a winner and never a loser. Lean: a podium of ribbons for every seat, the bots' too.
3. **The clock.** The grown-up rule says a timer is a second statistic and never ends a round; a placement bonus against bots is a relative clock. Lean: allowed, and labeled.
4. **Names and art.** Every row is re-themed (no Chomps, Goombas or Thwomps) under a generic name; rules and methods are not protected, but names and art can be.

**For the developer: which Mario Party games does the developer's wife play most?** *Super Mario Party*, *Superstars* and *Jamboree* have different minigame sets, and those favorites would reorder this list.

#### Touchscreen minigames in DS, Island Tour and Star Rush (Gemini's answer, checked)

*Pasted by the developer, 2026-10-11: Gemini's answer to prompt 4 below.* The three games' minigame lists give names and no controls, so I opened the individual page of every game Gemini named, plus a few it did not (each summarized by the fetch tool; **†** as above).

**Result.** Gemini named 27 games. **All 27 names are real and in the game it gave.** But **14 use the touchscreen, 10 use buttons or the Circle Pad, and 3 pages say nothing** (they are disambiguations), and for four of the 14 it gave the wrong action. Its "stroke the sides of the screen to paddle" claim was attached to the wrong games: Tragic Carpet Ride and Sub Trouble use the Circle Pad. Its list of games for small children rests on claims that do not hold for at least three of its seven (Cheep Cheep Chance is a luck pick with the pad, Airbrushers is a winged coin game on the A button, and Goomba Guess is a memory game that takes a point off for a wrong tap), so I would not use it as a suitability guide.

**The 14 that do use touch** (opened), with what Gemini said where it differs:

| Game (title) | What its page says | Gemini's claim |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Trace Cadets (DS) | Stylus only; draw shapes on your own grid "without lifting your stylus"; the first to fill their square wins | Right; "punishes going off-line" is not on the page |
| Hedge Honcho (DS) | Stylus clears leaves from your path across five hedges, rubs off ladybugs, avoids stunning bees; first to the pond | "Tap targets": wrong gesture |
| Goomba Wrangler (DS) | Draw a full circle with the stylus round wandering Goombas; 20 seconds; circling a Bob-omb halves your catch | Right (lasso) |
| Twist and Route (DS) | Draw clockwise circles to wind a mini-car, then steer with the pad or buttons, rewinding when it slows | Right, but it also needs steering buttons |
| Soil Toil (DS, 2 against 2) | **Each teammate spins one front wheel with the stylus, and the car moves toward the slower wheel** | Right about spinning; misses the two-person split |
| Rail Riders (DS) | Slide the stylus up the screen to build speed down a handrail; the higher the slide, the farther the jump | "Drag tracks or levers": wrong action |
| Cherry-Go-Round (DS) | Move the stylus for five seconds to spin cherries, then throw; farthest wins, capped at 200 | "Tap to rotate or pluck": wrong |
| Rotisserie Rampage (DS, 1 against 3) | The solo player spins a slab with the stylus to knock the team off (hostile) | Right |
| Grid is Good (Island Tour) | Drag trominoes onto a grid in 10 seconds; a number two players claim burns out | "Tap tiles": partly |
| Puzzle Pronto (Island Tour) | One of four touch puzzles; "Touch or slide to complete the puzzle" | Plausible; the details are not on the page |
| Tile Savvy (Island Tour) | Slide tiles to rebuild a scrambled picture; first to finish three puzzles | Right |
| Goomba Guess (Star Rush) | Memorize where 12 Goombas and nine Galoombas shuffle out of sight, then tap the spots; a point for each right tap, one off for each wrong | Right about tapping; misses the memory and the penalty |
| Splat-a-Stamp (Star Rush) | "Tap the paintball, slide it down, and then release to launch" at a stamp; scored by percent of ink coverage, and others can cover your ink | "Drag a stamp into place": wrong action |
| Bridgesaw Puzzle (Star Rush) | Tap a piece and slide it right to build a bridge; three rounds of three to five pieces; 5, 3, 2, 1 by finish | Right |

**Not in Gemini's list:** **Paddle Skedaddle** (Island Tour) races canoes down a creek, and the page lists the stylus, used to paddle, as the only control (a demo shows paddling left and right; its strokes and steering rule are not on the page). Search excerpts†: Starring Artist (copy a constellation on the touchscreen), Tap Dash, or Find It First in the British version (tap the matching panel in a 16-panel grid), Cucumberjacks (swipe to slice a vegetable). **Buttons or pad, not touch:** Airbrushers, Cheep Cheep Chance, Study Fall, Camera Shy and Globe Gunners (DS), Rope a Slope, Tragic Carpet Ride and Sub Trouble (Island Tour), Pop Quiz and Tiles and Tribulations (Star Rush), and Memory Mash (DS), which Gemini did not name. **Not described:** Dress for Success, Dust Buddies and Domino Effect.

**What the checked list adds.**

- **Paddling on a touchscreen has a precedent** in Paddle Skedaddle, which supports [Row Together](#candidates-for-two)'s input. Its steering rule is not on the page, so Gemini's "stroking the left side turns right" stays unverified.
- **Soil Toil is a verified two-person split on touch**, Making Faces' idea again: each partner owns one wheel and the slower wheel decides the way, so matching speeds goes straight, the rowing rule in a wheel. It supports Row Together and Two-Knob Doodle.
- **Gestures the notebook already plans have precedents:** drawing circles to wind (Twist and Route) for the [crank](#gestures-the-island-does-not-use-yet), circling to lasso (Goomba Wrangler) for Lasso Loops, sliding up for power (Rail Riders) and a slingshot launch (Splat-a-Stamp) for Bouncy Launch, sliding tiles and select-and-slide pieces (Tile Savvy, Bridgesaw Puzzle) for Puzzle Pals. Sesame's finding that flicking is hard for developing hands applies to the slingshot.
- **For small children, apply the island's own rules, not the series' ratings:** tap, slide, circle and rub suit a four-year-old; tracing without lifting, flicking, spinning for a count and alternating strokes are the hard ones.
- **One new seed: Slide Picture** (Tile Savvy, and the classic sliding-tile toy). Slide a tile into the gap to rebuild a picture drawn in code (Puzzle Pals' render-to-texture cutting). A board is made by sliding randomly from the solved picture, so it is always solvable (a test), and a search gives the fewest slides as par and a hint; 2 by 3 for pre-K up to 4 by 4 for grown-ups. S · island pre-K to school and grown-up cozy. Number Nook now builds the grown-up numbered sliding-board interaction; rebuilding a picture and younger-band boards remain proposals.

#### Keep Talking and barrier games (Gemini's answer, checked)

*Pasted by the developer, 2026-10-11: Gemini's answer to prompt 6.* I opened [Wikipedia's page on the game](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keep_Talking_and_Nobody_Explodes); barrier games I saw only in search excerpts†, and I opened no speech-therapy source.

**Result.** The account of *Keep Talking* **matches Wikipedia**: a Defuser sees the bomb and the Experts hold the manual; neither can see the other's side, and they talk across a table; modules can be disarmed in any order; a countdown and a strike limit end a bomb, and strikes speed the timer; the page names homophones, glyphs and common words such as "press" and "left" as what makes the talking hard. (The page does not say whether the manual is printed, a PDF or web-only, and it names no untimed mode.) The account of **barrier games** matches the excerpts†: two people behind a folder or screen, one giving instructions and the other following by words alone, with clarification prompt cards ("Can you explain?", "Do you mean here or there?") and the adult modeling clarification first. Neither source gives an age range, and the research I found is small (a 1989 study of 42 upper-elementary children and a study with autistic adolescents and young adults†), so **no claim of language benefit** is made here.

**Its adaptation, "Secret Agent & HQ", is a plain barrier game with a spy theme**: a folder, six to ten bricks each, no screen. The child builds a secret structure and describes it piece by piece, the parent builds from the words and asks clarifying questions, then the barrier comes down and they compare. It meets the house rules (no clock, no strikes, "signal interference" in place of "wrong", roles swap). Two cautions: its roles are muddled (it gives Mission Control "a blueprint guide" while the child holds the secret), and it **leaves out the half of *Keep Talking* that makes it a brain game**: a manual of rules in which what you see must be matched to an action. That half is what an app can add, a gadget and a manual that always agree because they come from one seed.

**What it adds.**

- **It resolves the blocker on [Lantern Keeper's Manual](#candidates-for-two).** Hidden information cannot be kept on one iPad, but a physical barrier can keep it, with the secret on a card or in a build behind a folder. *Keep Talking* itself keeps its manual outside the screen, and it ships on phones and tablets (iOS and Android, 2019).
- **One new seed: Mission Cards** (a barrier game). The app deals seeded blueprint cards drawn in code from the island's shapes, colors and critters, shown to the describer or printed from the grown-ups' page (plain HTML) with a mission number, while the builder uses real blocks or paper. The reveal is the check, so nothing needs a machine's verdict. Ladder: two pieces with a color and one position word ("on top of", "next to"); four pieces with size; six pieces with left and right from the builder's side and one piece "behind"; then swap roles and have the builder say it back. A mismatch is "signal interference", never a miss. The pet, as a radio operator, voices the clarifying questions for the builder to copy ("Do you mean here or there?"), the prompt cards of therapy in character. The vocabulary is a claim to check with a speech-language source before a card says what it teaches. **A variant, Where Am I?** (after Wii Party U's Lost-and-Found Square): the describer holds a card showing the view from one spot ("a big tree on my left, a red house in front") and the other finds that spot on the island map on the iPad; the position words and the reveal are the same. Neighbors: Treasure Map, Peekaround Island, Bug Builder. S (printable) to M (with the pet) · island 6 to 11 and a family-table row.

#### Team and co-op minigames across the series (Gemini's answer, checked)

*Pasted by the developer, 2026-10-11: Gemini's answer to prompt 1.* Gemini declined to list every minigame ("hundreds of entries") and gave six summaries by era, so prompt 1's per-minigame list is still open. I opened the Star Rush, Kaboom Squad and Jamboree TV pages, the 2-vs-2 sections of twelve games' minigame lists (the ninth game's page has none), and ten minigame or board pages (each summarized by the fetch tool; **†** as above).

**The count.** "Well over 120 team (2-vs-2) minigames" **holds, but only just, on the wiki's lists:** there are **127 two-against-two minigames** in the games that have the category (the first game 5; games 2 to 8 have 12, 10, 9, 12, 12, 12 and 10; the tenth has 10; *DS* 13; *Super Mario Party* 10; *Jamboree* 12; the ninth game, *Island Tour* and *Star Rush* have none, and *Superstars* and *The Top 100* only remake older ones). A minigame is listed again in each game it returns to (Jamboree's Defuse or Lose and Jump the Gun are also in the fifth and sixth games' lists), so this counts listings, not distinct minigames. *Super Mario Party*'s separate sets of 10 team and 10 co-op minigames make **147**, and the ten in Kaboom Squad's pool **157**. *Jamboree TV* adds 14 mouse minigames that can be played two against two or all four together.

**What held and what did not.**

- **Held:** *Super Mario Party*'s ten co-op names and River Survival's description; Home on the 'Rang (hurl boomerangs at Pokeys with a Joy-Con, 30 seconds, hits after time still count); Cake Factory (one places a cake, the partner adds the strawberry; 30 seconds); Dungeon Dash (each teammate controls one leg and they must move together); Kaboom Squad's five rounds.
- **Wrong:** Star Rush's bosses are **not cooperative**. Toad Scramble is competitive, boss fights are open to anyone who taps A to join and players "Nab Stars by getting 1st place", and the page has no Boss Rush mode. Rocket Road is a **competitive** board (25 spaces, Boosters multiply the dice, the first to finish wins) with no rocket or fuel. Kaboom Squad's pool lists **ten** minigames, not nine, and about five are played per run. Penguin Pushers is penguin herding with the control stick, not pushing a block. Handcar Havoc has no split roles (steer by leaning, brake with B, speed up by mashing A). Jamboree TV's Tag Team Rules make **teams that share** coins, Stars and items and compete, with a Together Dice both partners roll and move the sum of; it is not co-op against the game.
- **No source for** its ratings of whether a parent and young child could play together. They are Gemini's opinion; the pages show almost every one is timed (30 to 60 seconds, or a draw at three to five minutes).

**What the opened pages add: verified two-person patterns.**

| Pattern | Minigame (opened) | Where it lands on the island |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Joint attention**: both must pick the same thing | Etch 'n' Catch (MP3, Superstars): each teammate circles a moving picture on their side with a crayon; when both circle the same one it is freed; five frees win | A two-seat mode of Lasso Loops: a friend is caught only when both loops close round it (below) |
| **Hand-off** | Picking Panic (MP3): one teammate grabs cherries and tosses them, the other puts them in a basket; cherry size sets how far they fly | The Pitch and Catch idea as a two-seat Egg Catch |
| **Production line** | Cake Factory (MP2): one places the cake, the partner puts the strawberry on top | Pet Kitchen and Scoop Shop for two (already a row below) |
| **One leg each** | Dungeon Dash (MP2, a remake of the first game's Desert Dash): each teammate controls one leg and they must move at the same time | The three-legged race: the **Two-Leg Walk** seed below |
| **Slower wheel decides** | Soil Toil (DS) | Row Together and Two-Knob Doodle |
| **Rhythm with a fallback** | Rhythm Recital (Star Rush): up to four play songs on the touchscreen or A button; CPU players fill empty slots and have no effect on the performance; the only cooperative mode on the page | The pet taking the other seat: the series' own solo-fallback rule, for a song |
| **A boss that is a chore** | Mega Goomba's Bad Dream (Star Rush boss): take apples from a sleepy boss, three at a time, to a box; 50 wins; points come off when the boss connects or a player hits a Goomba; a draw at five minutes | A big friend's chore as the finale of a board |
| **Pair the leader with the trailer** | Balloon Bash (Star Rush): a boss fight puts first and fourth place against second and third | A parent and child are that pair by construction |
| **Rescue counts** | Kaboom Squad: a hit player floats in a bubble and a teammate frees them; the final star rating counts players rescued | A "helper" ribbon wherever a game has a rescue |

The series also has a short board: Island Tour's Rocket Road takes **10 to 20 minutes** (25 spaces, a minigame every third turn), the range the Island Party Board aims for.

#### Wii Party's off-screen modes (Gemini's answer, checked)

*Pasted by the developer, 2026-10-11: Gemini's answer to prompt 3.* I opened Wikipedia's pages on *Wii Party* and *Wii Party U*; all four pages of Nintendo's *Iwata Asks: Wii Party*, read on its UK site because the Iwata Asks site itself returned 503; reviews of the sequel by Nintendo Life, Nintendo World Report, Outcyders and Kill Screen; a Gaming Nexus preview of the first game; and the Family Gaming Database's page on the sequel (each summarized by the fetch tool, which caps its quotations, so every quotation here is the tool's; **†** as above). Giant Bomb (403) and two fan wikis (402) would not open, so Friend Connection's "one picks an answer with the D-pad and the other guesses it" stays a search excerpt†.

**Result.** Every game name is real, and the mechanics Gemini gave are close to the pages' own. Its slips are one misfiling, some staging and examples that are its own, and a good deal left out. Its four reasons for why the modes suit a parent and child have no source of their own.

- **Held:** Hide 'n' Hunt (one player hides one to four remotes, and "every 10 seconds, each Wii Remote will produce an animal sound"); Animal Tracker (the remotes make animal sounds and only one matches the TV picture); Time Bomb (one remote passed round; shaking it or pressing the wrong button explodes it); Word Bomb (say a word from the category before passing); Button Smashers as a Twister (Nintendo Life: "a finger-based version of Twister"; Nintendo World Report: "pretty much co-op Twister"); Water Runners (scoop at the GamePad, carry it steady to the TV; Outcyders: you "walk" with the remote held steady); Friend Connection (a quiz, points when answers match, a rating of the pair); Buddy Quiz's chosen Buddy; Balance Boat (cooperative, two players, Miis balanced on a ship, two-player minigames).
- **A misfiling, following my prompt.** Prompt 3 asked about Wii Party U's "Pair and Buddy modes", and Gemini filed Do U Know Mii? under them and described it with Friend Connection. **Wii Party U has no Pair or Buddy mode** (its modes are TV Party, House Party, GamePad Party and Minigame Mode). Do U Know Mii? is a House Party quiz for three or four players about one player's personality, guessed on a sliding scale (Kill Screen: it "most resembles *The Newlywed Game*"), not a pair's compatibility test.
- **Staging and examples that are Gemini's own, on no page:** the remotes lined up on a floor or table and the "ambient sounds" in Animal Tracker; closed eyes and a cat or dog in Hide 'n' Hunt; Water Runners' table across the room, the run to the GamePad, a remote dipped into the touch screen like a ladle, and a pitcher (the pages say a creek on the GamePad and a jar or jug at the TV); the circle in Time Bomb; and the "$20" question and "Types of Fruit" category, which it gave as examples. **Unverified:** Balance Boat's "must beat … to keep the characters small and easier to balance". Gaming Nexus says the pair's minigame performance decides which Miis they get, that their size and position affect balance, and that the game ends if the ship tips; it does not say whether small is easier.
- **Left out:** that every Animal Tracker remote makes a noise and the first to 3 points wins; that Friend Connection's five questions come *before* a cooperative minigame and it needs two human players; Balance Boat's twenty Miis and the computer partner; the scoring of House Party, which is competitive (the Hide 'n' Hunt hider earns the seconds each seeker took; whoever holds Word Bomb at the blast loses); Quick Draw (a real deck of cards, Japan and Korea only); and five more House Party games in the sequel: Name That Face, Lost-and-Found Square, Sketchy Situation, Feed Mii! and Dance With Mii. **Player counts matter for one parent and one child.** Wikipedia calls Buddy Quiz the only House Party game in the first game that needs three or four players, which implies the others take two (inferred); in the sequel five of eight need three or four, and only Button Smashers, Water Runners and Dance With Mii take two (Family Gaming Database).
- **No source for** its four reasons as written (a level skill floor, physical play, icebreakers, cooperation). The producers' words below support the aims behind two (fairness; the room as the play space), hoped parents and children would try Friend Connection, and aimed Buddy Quiz at a different audience. No review discusses children: Nintendo Life says the sequel was built "with everyone — even your Grandma — in mind", Outcyders' reviewers play it with "the little ones through to the grandparents", and the Family Gaming Database sets its skill level at 6+ (that site's opinion).

**What the producers said** (*Iwata Asks: Wii Party*: Iwata with Ikeda, Nd Cube's producer; its director Nishiya; and Nintendo's Hirose, an associate producer, and Sato, a producer. **Page 1 is about the Mario Party series**; pages 2 to 4 are about *Wii Party*.)

On Mario Party (page 1; the page does not say whether the remarks also apply to the new game):

- **Fair for mixed ages.** Nishiya: "One thing we put effort into was making a game that would be fair for everyone, so that when family members play together, parents won't have to lose on purpose for their children to win." Hirose, who says playing video games is not a strength, wanted "a game about which anyone could say 'Even I can play this game'", where "even I can win sometimes". Iwata: "There is an element of chance, so one good player won't win all the time." This is the team's own account of luck as the leveler; the notebook's earlier source was a *Mario Party 10* producer's comment†.
- **Rules for the holidays.** Nishiya: the team had always wanted "a game that families could enjoy together during the holidays, so we needed rules that would be easy for anyone to understand."
- **The dice are the identity, and ideas are kept in stock.** The team thought of "not using dice"; Iwata: "if you don't use dice, then some might say it's not Mario Party." Ikeda: the team comes up with "hundreds of minigame ideas" and uses those that fit; Nishiya: they "kept a stock of ideas."
- **Not on the page:** why a board plus minigames, bonus stars, handicaps, controls and practice screens. Prompt 2 is answered for luck and fairness only.

On Wii Party (pages 2 to 4):

- **The room is the board.** Iwata: "The whole room becomes the play space", and House Party "is fun for a change of tempo because of the contrast between moving and sitting still." Hirose: "The first minigame we thought of was Hide 'n' Hunt", after a colleague "quietly said, 'What if we hid the Wii Remote?'" (reported by Nishiya).
- **No handicap.** Ikeda: "we made it a party game that grandmas and grandpas can join and play without the need for a handicap". The island reaches the same end by adapting her level and never showing a handicap.
- **Who the pair modes suit.** Nishiya, on Friend Connection: "I hope young couples or married couples or parents and their children will try it out", and "The results aren't anything you should take too seriously." Hirose, on Buddy Quiz: "rather than children, I think it's really fun for younger generations, like students and people in their twenties to play with their friends." So the designers pointed Friend Connection at parents and children and Buddy Quiz at friends; neither is evidence of what a young child enjoys.
- **Tuning a steadiness game.** Nishiya, on Time Bomb: "everyone passed the remote around so carefully that the bomb never exploded!" So the team added "a time limit to rush the players", buttons to press on the screen, and "the vibrations on the screen as a graph, making it really easy to see how steady you are." The island has no round-ending timer, so a steady-hands game there takes the graph and a glow for calm, not the rush.
- **Two rules the island shares.** Iwata: Miis "represent you, your family, and your friends", and "It isn't allowed to do anything unpleasant to the Mii characters in order to get a laugh from the players" (the pet's rule here). When colleagues tested the game, Nishiya: "The biggest thing was tempo", for example "an overview of the course in about five seconds" and a start-up sequence before a race; the island's how-to card already skips "again" and has the grown-up's switch.
- **Where the island differs.** Ikeda: "you can tell at a glance who won and who lost"; Iwata: "there's no room for excuses." The island shows nobody as the loser.

**Verified off-screen patterns, and where they land.**

| Pattern | Game (opened) | Where it lands |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **A hider and a beacon** | Hide 'n' Hunt: one to four remotes hidden, each chirping an animal every ten seconds; seekers have a time limit | The **Chime Hunt** seed, now with the ten-second chirp, and **Count and Hide**, where the screen counts and no device is hidden. The hider's score is dropped |
| **Listening to choose among objects** | Animal Tracker: every remote makes a noise, one matches the picture; grab it; first to 3 | Needs several sound-making objects at once and the island has one iPad. Sound Garden and Peekaboo Barn hold what one screen can do |
| **Pass it round** | Time Bomb (pass while holding the shown button; shake it and it explodes) and Word Bomb (name something in the category) | **Pass the Pet**, with no bomb: when the tune stops, the holder takes the turn. A steadiness meter for Tilt Together |
| **Carry it steady across the room** | Water Runners: scoop at the GamePad's creek, walk with the remote steady, deposit at the TV; two to four players | An off-screen cousin of Fill and Dump's pouring: a spoon of water to carry, with the screen as announcer |
| **Many hands on one set of buttons** | Button Smashers: up to four remotes and the GamePad, "co-op Twister" | Parachute Pals' unison with three or four fingers; Bounce Back's two-touch pattern |
| **Two views of one place** | Lost-and-Found Square: one player, disguised as a pet, tilts the GamePad to see the place and describes it while the others search the level; praised by Nintendo Life and Nintendo World Report; three or four players | **Where Am I?**, a variant of Mission Cards |
| **Guess what the other will pick** | Friend Connection (the designers hoped parents and children would try it), Buddy Quiz (aimed at friends "rather than children") and Do U Know Mii? (Newlywed-style) | **Same Pick**, whose ladder already starts with favorites |
| **Take the order** | Feed Mii!: one player is the employee who places the food the others order | The Market Stall and Pet Kitchen two-seat modes |
| **Balance together** | Balance Boat: a pair earns pieces in two-player minigames and places them on a mast; a computer can be the partner | A **Block Tower** two-seat mode; the series' solo-fallback rule again |

**What it adds.** The Mario Party team's own words on fairness and chance (prompt 2, partly), two seeds (Count and Hide, Pass the Pet), a Block Tower two-seat mode, a Where Am I? variant of Mission Cards, a better Chime Hunt row, and a sixth item in the suggested order. Set aside: Word Bomb's loser; the Hide 'n' Hunt hider's score; Sketchy Situation's bluff (one player draws a different prompt and the rest spot who), a different game for an older band; and Name That Face, which photographs a face (none of the island's code asks for a camera; Feelings Faces' mirror faces cover the idea off screen). The island's contract stays two seats: only Count and Hide and Pass the Pet reach a circle of three or more.

#### Order and decisions for the family table

**Suggested order (non-binding).**

1. **Two-seat modes that reuse a solver:** Secret Code's maker and breaker, then Penguin Slide with split directions. Each is a mode of a tested game and proves the seat contract (adaptation, credit, solo fallback, two touches) before any new game does.
2. **Same Pick:** no new engine, and the clearest fit for "kid-driven".
3. **Row Together's lap and toddler slice:** she is about one, and a grown-up-led rowing song is the first thing here she can use; it also builds the two-lane steering function.
4. **The Island Party Board's mini board** (two dice, a path, cheers), then its minigame stops.
5. **Skip Rope**, then **Two-Knob Doodle**, then **Tilt Together**; **Lantern Keeper's Manual** last, after the second-screen decision.
6. **Off-screen seeds when a small hour is free:** Count and Hide, Pass the Pet and Chime Hunt need voice lines and a music stop, not an engine, and the grown-up leads at her age.

**Decisions this needs from the developer.** (Also under [Waiting on the developer](WAITING-ON-DEVELOPER.md#waiting-on-the-developer).)

1. **Competition on the island.** Lean: cooperative by default; a competitive mode only where luck or a visible handicap evens the match and nobody is ever shown as the loser (ribbons for every seat; ties score for both, as the couch already does).
2. **Where these games live.** (a) A land of games made for two, working name **Pal Plaza**, which must pass the land-fit test at every band and find a spot on a map that now holds ten lands, a home spot and the picnic; (b) a "two seats" mark on any game that has such a mode, with no new land; (c) Game night in Lantern Lake (Lands stage E) for the party board. A game belongs to exactly one land, so a game in (a) is not also in a subject land. Lean: (a) for games made for two (Row Together, Two-Knob Doodle, Same Pick, Skip Rope), (b) for modes, and (c) for the board.
3. **The second seat's contract.** Anonymous and uncredited, with only seat 1's role read by `difficulty.ts` and a role-swapped round counting as supported completion; or a profile in every seat at stage E. Lean: the first, now.
4. **Kept things made together** ("our picture", "our song", a puzzle one made for the other). A kept thing needs a bounded creation kind with repair, migration and backup tests, the same question as the [family album](#new-ideas-from-jumpstart). Lean: no kept puzzles; one "our picture" slot only if the developer wants it.
5. **Table mode and a second screen.** The iPad flat between two people, and a printed or second-screen manual, each change the layout or the contract. A physical barrier and a printed card avoid both ([Mission Cards](#keep-talking-and-barrier-games-geminis-answer-checked)). Lean: not now for the layout; Mission Cards first for hidden information.
